• A MEAN STATEMENT.—She: "How do you think I will look when I am real old ? " He : "Much the same as you do now." (At first she was pleased, then she got mad, and finally she didn't exactly know how to feel about it.) "Any money in his clothes?" asked the first burglar. "Nothing but three pennies and a lot of ribbon," replied the second with disgust. " Rats ! You've got his wife's rational dress." ONE evening Tom Sheridan, sitting with his father over a bottle, was complaining of the empti- ness of his pocket. The right honourable manager 'told him jocularly to go on the highway. "I have tried that already," said he, "but without success." " Aye ! how !" replied his father. "Why," resumed he, "1 stoped a caravan full of passengers, who assured me they had not a far- thing, as they all belonged to Drury Lane Theatre, and could not get a penny of their salary."

THE CAREFUL BOER. —An amusing tale comes from Johannesburg. In a time of peace and " boom " a burgher walked into a well-known banking-house, and somewhat surprised the clerk by presenting a draft on his own account for £lo,ooo—the precise amount of his balance. After a little delay the cash was paid to him over the counter. He counted the coins very carefully, and then pushed them back towards the clerk, observing—" I don't want them. I only wished to make sure they were there."

ON one occasion Sir Henry Irving, entering a a train at King's Cross, London, found that four passengers already occupied the corners of the carriage, and linfl appropriated the rest of the seats for their portmanteaus. As no one moved Sir Henry continued to stand, holding on to the hat. rail. After a while, one of the passengers sulkily began to move his luggage from the seat, seeing which Sir Henry remarked in his blandest tone, "Oh, please don't 13t me disturb you. I'm get- -ting out at Scotland." CAUSE FOR DISSATISFACTION.—UncIe Gilchrist: "I am tired and sick of these encycloptedias—tired and sick of 'em. I'll send 'em back to the pub- lishers to-morrow." Niece: "Way, uncle, what is the matter with the encyclopwdias ?" Uncle Gilchrist: " Matter ? Why, I want to know something about the aesars, and I can't find a word on the subject. The encyclopo3dia ignores the 'whole kit of 'em." Niece: Have you looked, uncle ?" Uncle Gilchrist: " Looked ? Of course I have looked. I've been through every page of the S's." _ _

" CAPITAL " ORA T.

Next session is likely to see an end to an experi- ment which lent a certain interest to the slack time in tbe past session. It is whispered that the wait- resses who adorned the Terrace are no longer to be allowed in that august spot. The reasons which are adduced are manifold, but there can be little doubt that the real reason is that the experiment led to an intolerable amount of small scandal, especially eut- side Parliament. If the waitresses are excluded, it will be a serious loss to the young bloods of the Tory party, whose periodical games of football on the Terrace were played in the presence of - these fair spectators. I have never seen such an influx of the fairer sex into the lobbies of the two Houses as that which last session witnessed. Some of the more serious members were actually so ungallant as to complain.

There is something mysterious about the tremen- dous activity with which money is just now being shovelled, if one may say so, into public-house property in London. Vast sums have been and are being spent on purchasing and rebuilding—how much exactly it is difficult to say, but the lease and rebuilding of a fully licensed but very small house in the South of London have just cost .230,000. Have the publicans a hint of some such boon from the presant Government as Mr. Goschen tried to grant them during the last Tory reign?

From certain remarks made by Viscount Peel prior to his departure for Geneva there need be no apprehension that the appointment of the Licensing Commission will furnish an excuse for indefinitely postponing legislation relating to the drink traffic. The view taken by the ex-Speaker and shared by some of the most influential of his fellow-Commis- sioners is that, whatever conclusions may be arrived at as the result of the inquiry, they should be stated in plain, unequivocal language, and that if the weight of evidence is against continuing the present system the Commissioners should formulate an alternative scheme. It may be taken for granted therefore, that when the time comes for preparing his draft report Lord Peel will spare no labour in analysing the eviuence and working out the logical deductions, and in any event his impartiality may be taken for granted.

. Lord Londonderry has given voice to the dissatis- faction that has been smouldering among the rank and file of the Tories since the Home Secretary opened the prison doors to several of the Irish pri- soners. Having already tasted the sweets of re- bellion, and gained some notoriety thereby,his lord- ship speaks aloud what is only muttered or spoken in undertones in the Tory clubs, it is to be pre- sumed that Lord Londonderry has read the accounts of the condition of the men who have been set at liberty, and yet, notwithstanding this, he calls aloud Lr the full pound of flesh. Events have so far vindicated the Home Secretary's action, and borne out the medical report of the mental collapse of the prisoners. Die most bigoted of mortals, it might have been thought, would not desire to vin- dictively insist on the full measure of a sentence after the men who were undergoing it had become it for nothing but a lunatic asylum. What requires t-zplanation is not why the Home Secretary released them, but why the medical officers allowed them so hopelessly to break down without reporting their condition. The lurid light which their case throws , upon our prison administration will strengthen the I arguments of those who have long demanded its I reform.

The War Office has been questioned as to the statement made this week that Dr. Jameson's brother officers are not, after all, to lose their corn - missions. Short of actually confirming this state- ment, it may be doubted whether the War Office coqld say more than it does. The announcement is described as non-official and premature. But what of that if the War Office refuses to stigmatise it as incorrect? The fact is, of course, that after the commutations of the sentences upon Doctor Jameson and his fellow-prisoners, the decision in the case'of the officers concerned was never really in doubt. The authorities had evidently made up their minds to let the offenders down as gently as possible. Their policy may or may not be in accord- ance with the general drift of public opinion, but many shrewd observers are disposed to doubt the prudence of it. The gist of the matter is that the War Office, being in a position to inflict punish - meat, has declined to do so. What sort of an im- pression is this decision likely to create, and what sort of an impression ought it to create?

Prince Ranjitsinbji's complimentary dinner is going to be a big affair. Among those who have now signified their intention of doing honour to the head of the batting averages are Professor Jebb end Lord Flarris. All the leading counties are re- presented on the dinner Committee,and the leading Cambridge cricketers are taking an active part in the preparations for what promises to be a highly interesting function. Thiecordiality is the more to be appreciated because in certain quarters, especi- ally in London, Raujitsinhji's success was not very enthusiastically received for some time. Be has forced his way to the front by sheer skill in the teeth of a good deal of rather discreditable pre- judice. Outside London, as so often happens, a more generous spirit was displayed from the firet.

London is fairly stirred by the condition of Con. stantinople, and a huge protest meeting would enable it to speak with effect, if any meetings can reflect with efficiency the condemnation the world feels. Several preparatory steps have been taken. The Duke of Argyll was invited to take part in the demonstration, but though he has written to show his syrapathy,he suggests that the chairman should be a non-political person. This has brought about an invitation to the Bishop of Rochester,which has gone to him at Hawarden. There is the fainte.t hope that Mr. Gladstone may send a message or letter to the meeting. Very little is needed to light a mighty flame of protest, spreading like Wildfire through the land. The new movement of depor- tation is hardly le.s cruel than downright massacre, for it means death by starvation to many families leaving Constantinople.

The Zanzibar slavery question seems not unlikely to be pressed upon us from an unpected quarter. The Germans in East Africa are doing their best to gradually extinguish slavery. A report to Berlin on the, subject sets forth that the stopping of tbe trade in slaves from German East Africa will only be pos- sible when the protecting Power in Zanzibar—that is, England—determines to proceed enerczeticallY against the importation of slaves into Zanzibar and Pemha. The slave mirket will thus be closed, and slave raiding will of itself come !o an end. Major Von Wiest:flan, the German governor, reports that, slaves are attached as porters and wives of porters to caravans coastward bound, and are so described in ti!e permits issued to the caravan leaders by the superintendents of interior stations. More atten- tion is to he given to these permits. A jog from Germany on the slave question would be very ugly just now.

A surprise is being prepared for the Shrewsbury Church Congress. A hand of young men will go down, hold on air meetings, and exhibit. certain instruments of torture now used by advanced illergymen of the Church of England for personal discipline in the fcirm of the " ,tu-red knout?' the "br;tplate," the "steel discipline," and the "horse- hair belt." These articles are now to be seen in a booksellers shop in Paternoster-row, and have been inspected by scoffing and cynical crowds. It is Aeriously stated that such things are in actual use, but are rarely seen by the public because they are regarded a:-± sacred..

_ _ bing. rfut I wanted to belong to you, as I sup- posed I did. And I have no claim on you at all. I belong to that hateful creature at the inn." "No, you don't," replied the squire folding her closer. "You belong to me, and I shall never let you go from me except to brighten some good man's home. A few pounds will buy the silence and the absence of Mrs. Narr."

_ "But it won't buy back my lost happiness I" cried Dora. "I could have smiled at poverty—l could have borne griefs and misfortunes—but this!" "Did you comply with her demands, Dora? Did you go to the inn?' "Yes, papa, I went there, and I saw Mrs. Narr. She told me a long story—how you and mamma saw me by the road-side, and how you took a fancy to me and how you bought me of her. I tried to disbelieve it, and all the while my fears told me that she spoke the truth I can't believe that lam her daughter, papa. If there is anything in instinct I -ought to have felt kindly toward her. But I didn't. She seemed a decent woman enough, but she and I are no more alike than day and night." "That is what my wife said," said the squire, still in his husky voice. "She could not believe that you were the woman's child, and held to it that you were of another breed entirely !" "But if I were, that would bring me no nearer to you, papa," said Dora, wistfully. "Mrs. Narr told me that she had the child of a noble family to nurse while I was an infant, and that the child died just before her flight--" " It's just as likely her own died, and that she kept the nurse-child, for some reason or other of her own!" cried the squire, vehemently. "I would believe anything, rather than believe you to 'be her daughter. It is quite possible—" "But not very probable!" interposed Dora. "Why should a fleeing couple embarrass them- selves with a child not their own? If they had lost their own little one, they would have left the nurse-child behind to be restored to its parents. Papa," she added, abruptly, "has this woman any legal claim upon me? Can she compel me to go away with her ? " "All she wants is money," replied the squire, evasively. "I shall buy her off to-morrow, and send her away, so that you will not see her again." Dora pressed his hand gratefully. She had been stunned by the terrible discovery that she was not the squire's daughter, but already her buoyant nature was rising from its first im- pression, and she was beginning to look the fact full in the face and to make the best of it.

As may therefore be guessed, Dora was not one to give way to sentimental repining. She had a brave and resolute soul, and all its forces la ere aroused to meet this heavy trial upon her. If she was not the squire's daughter by right of birth, she was by the diviner law of love and adop- tion. He would not let her be taken from him, and he shared equally in the anguish her discovery had coat her. She had lapsed into a thoughtful mood, when the squire asked: "Is Mrs. Narr over at the Hare and Hounds now?"

Dora answered in the affirmative. "I'll step over and see her in the morning," declared the squire. "The secret must be a secret still. My poor wife adopted a good many precau- tions—such as a long stay abroad and in the North of England—in order that no one might suspect you were not our own child. Edmund anew the truth, of course, and one or two others—among them the young squire whom I told in a confiden- tial mood at a time when I wished to test his love for you. "Then he knew the truth to-day when he asked me to marry him. The squire assented. Dora's cheeks flushed scarlet. "And he was willing to marry a nameless girl ?" she murmured. "He did not let me suspect that he ever guessed at the truth. I don't think I ever did full justice to Noel, papa. He has more chivalry than I imagined. To marry a nameless girl, with he knew not how many horrible relations some- where in the background, who would live upon him and disgrace him I I almost wish I had known it." "Would the knowledge have changed your decision, Dora ?" "N-no, papa—no !" "Did Warner offer himself to you to-day, dear ?" "Yes, father," was the whispered response. "And you told him—" "To speak to you, sir.", "Do you think he will stand the same test that Noel did ?" asked the squire, gently, letting his large brown hand flutter softly over the girl's short hair. "Will he be as willing to marry Squire Chessom's adopted daughter as Squire Chessom's own child? I spoke to you of the wide disparity of rank between Felix Warner, cousin and heir of Lord Champney, and Dora Chessom ? The dispar- ity is wider than you thought—" "Yes, papa," said the girl, bravely, "the dis. parity is great, but I will never marry any man who does not know the whole truth, and who does not think that Dora Chessom herself is worth more to him than another woman with rank, title, and boundless wealth. I will never allow any one to marry me with a feeling of pity either, or of superiority," she added, proudly. "I am as good now as I was yesterday—as good as if I were a princess! You understand me papa? I am not ashamed of my relationship to that poor woman at the inn, if she is honest and virtuous. I am ashamed of my relationship to her dead husband, for he was a drunkard, a forger, and a fugitive from justice. One need never blush for parents, however poor, if only they are worthy of respect." "I do not understand you, Dora 1" "I thought, perhaps, you would think me proud because I shrank from Mrs. Narr—because I can never think of her as my mother," said Dora, look- ing up into the squire's pale, worn face with an impassioned look. "It was not pride that held me back from her. We are different. Like oil and water we can never assimilate. But to speak again of Felix, papa," and Dora's voice grew lower. "I think he will stand the test. If he does not, I shall be glad to find him out in time."

"You will not be a dowerless bride, Dora," said Squire Chessom, tenderly. "All that I can settle upon you, I shall. I have not been well, lately, and knowing that I must leave nothing to chance in a case like this, I went over to see my lawyer to-day about making my will."

"Oh, father !" "As if I should die any sooner for having all anxieties off my mind, Fire-Fly !" said the squire, with an attempt at playfulness. " The fact is, I have been too dilatory about this matter. I ought to have provided for you years ago, knowing that Edmund is not of a generous--scarcely of a just— turn. My son likes money better than ever I did ;" and he sighed, as if the fact pained him. "But tbe lawyer, as I said, was not at home. I shall see him .to-morrow and draw up a will, leav- ing you co-heir with Edmund and quite an heiress in your ()wit right, I shall leave you eight thousand pounds—the same as if you were the child of my blood. I shall tell all this to Mr. Warner, since you wish him to know the truth. I want to see you married before I die, Dora. My little heiress would be a prize to Mrs. Narr, if there were no one to keep that woman at bay." "Does the doctor think you ill, papa ?" The squire hesitated. A look into the girl's pale, brave countenance seemed to settle any doubts he' might have entertained on the propriety of speaking freely to her.

" You have had a great deal to bear to-day, Dora," he said. 'Can you bear another shock ?"

_ Th,o girl gasped for breath. "It is not—not the hereditary heart disease ?" she stammered. • "I thought I was threatened with apoplexy," returned the squire. "This dizziness looked like it. But our old family doctor says—be brave, Dora—that I am not likely to die of apo- plexy, but that. I shall in all probability go, as my father and grandfather went, of heart disease. There, there, D ira I Hush, darling 1 I may live a score of years yet. I shall no doubt live. to see your children. Only the doctor warned me to beware of excitement, and to guard against it carefully, or he would not answer for the consequences." "Von have been cruelly excited to-night. You are excited now, papa." The squire smiled faintly. " A night's test will set me up again," he said. "Lean your head against me—so, darling. I want to think a little."

CHAPTER VII. LIKE A THIE7 IN THE NIGHT.

The sun was near its setting when Squire Ches. som rode within the open gates of the Grange, and halted at the porch, singing out lustily, as was his wont, for one of the stable-boys to come for his horse. The boy hurrying out, the squire slowly alighted upon the porch, and as slowly moved toward the open hall-door. He looked tired and jaded, his bluff and kindly face wore a deeper flush than ordinarily, and his motion, deliberate as it was, seemed to fatigue him. As he gained the threshold, Dora, radiant in a cool white dress, with a floating sash of crimson, came dancing down the broad stairs, as bright of face as if she had not been all day struggling with many and bitter anxieties, and as if at this very moment her heart were not aching with a dull pain. With the quick eyes of love, she detected the squire's jaded and troubled condition, and she hurried to him, taking his hat, and welcoming him after a blithe and sunny fashion peculiar to herself. "Now, papa," she said, in the soft, cooing way that the squire loved, and likened to the murmur of birds in spring, "you've been riding in the hot sun again, and are tired out. You ought to take me-along to take care of you—men are such thought- less creatures! No doubt you are hungry enough. Supper is all ready, or will be by the time you are dressed !"

The squire fancied that there was a deeper ten- derness than usual underlying Dora's gaiety, ...<1 that her eyes were even more loving than usual in their expression. " Ah, you've missed me to-day, you midget !" he cried, delightedly, kissing her. "And that Warner has been here too ! There's no one can quite fill the old father's place yet, eh, Dora ?" "Oh, no, no, indeed !" cried Dora, impetuously. "No one can ever fill your place to me, papa !" Her sunny face clouded over, and she broke from his grasp, running out upon the porch. "Bless her 1" murmured the squire, half inclined to pursue her. "The little witch was in a strange mood just then. Perhaps she refused Warner to- day 1 She'll tell me after supper. She has no secrets from me !"

He paused a moment, and then, as Dora did not return, he went up to his room. It was cool and shaded. His garments lay out upon the bed, ready for .use. Dora had placed everything to his hand; she was fond of looking after him, and waiting on him in a pretty, motherly fashion; and his bath was soon taken, and his toilet made. _ _

Then he descended in search of Dora. She was not on the porch—not in the drawing. room. After a little search he found her in the supper-room, a cool room with open windows, into which the hawthorn-scented air blew softly. . _

- The table was spread with a tempting repast of cold fowl, ripe strawberries, a pine-apple, a glass jug of yellow cream' a pot of fragrant tea, and delicious home-made bread. Dora took her seat behind the teapot, and proceeded to do the honours of the meal. _ _ _ _

"Did you get your expected letter from Edmund, papa ? " she asked, as the squire sat down heavily opposite her. "No, Dora. He must be very busy, or out of town temporarily. But I shall surely hear to-mor- row." . ..

_ "You got your pay for the sheep, papa ?" "Yes, Dora, and lodged it in the bank, all but an odd fifty pounds' which I reserved for s gift to a young lady friend of mine," said the squire, smil- ing. "There it is, Dora. You can buy yourself some finery with it. I believe that is what young ladies consider money made for—to buy fineries !" As he spoke he handed her over the table a little packet of ten crisp new Bank of England notes, the whole comprising the sum he had mentioned. "I don't need any money, papa," returned Dora. "I have plenty—" "Take it," interrupted the squire, with good- natured peremptoriness. "As it one could have too much money. You are not obliged to spend it, Dora, if you don't want it. Money don't spoil by keeping." Thus adjured, Dora took the money and crowded it into her little silver portemonnaie already of plethoric dimensions. " I shall have to go to Horsham again to- morrow," said the squire, after a little silence. "My lawyer was out of town to-day, but is expected home to-night. The doctor—" He paused abruptly, with a startled look at Dora. She caught his glance, and a sudden sense of alarm crept over her. "The doctor I" she repeated. "What of him, papa?" " Oh, nothing, nothing," returned the squire, with unnecessary haste, looking down at his plate. "I just called into the doctor's office for a tonic, or something of the sort. My dizziness, you know, Dora. I don't know how I came to mention the doctor's name. I must be growing stupid. Mr. Warner called, I suppose, this morning ?" Dora answered in the affirmative, blushing. "And the young squire—was he here ?" "He did not come in," said the girl, in a low voice. "We visited on the road, as he over- took me when I was coming home from a walk." "Did—did he say anything ?" asked the squire, eagerly. "Noel is a great favourite of mine, you know, Dora. Did he want to rob me of you 2"

"Yes, papa; but I told him it could not be. I love Noel as a brother, and in no other way." The squire sighed, and then, the meal being con- cluded, arose from the table, holding out his band to the girl. Dora sprang up and took it, and they passed into the sitting-room together. This was the family room, where the squire lounged through the heat of the day, while Dora sat by the great west windows, busy with her needle or crotchet-hook.

The room was high and square, furnished for summer use, with a delicately tinted paper on the walls, a few good paintings and proof engravings well hung, a yellow India matting on the floor, ad graceful bamboo furniture, light, yet strong. "Sit down here, papa," said Dora, leading him to a high-backed bamboo chair by the window. "I will sit here at your feet. I have something to tell you.,,

- She sat down on a hassock in the position she had indicated.

Something in her tone aroused the squire's curiosity. He looked narrowly into her upturned face, and for the first time since his return noticed that she was pale, and that under all her bright- ness and gaiety she had a weary and sorrowful look. _ _ _

"What is it, Dora?" he asked, with fatherly tenderness. "What troubles you, darling? Is it these lovers of your.!?"

"Oh, no, papa, Dora murmured. "I only wish I might have no greater trouble than that of choosing between two suitors. Read that, father." She drew from her pocket the note she had received that morning from Mrs. Narr. It was crumpled now, but Dora •straightened it over her knees, pressing out the wrinkles, and then silently gave it into the hands of the squire. After a moment's search for his speCtacles, the squire commenced reading the letter, a dazed and bewildered expression on his face gradually deepening into a look of horror and consternation. He read it through and again, as Dora had done. Then also, as Dora had done, he crumpled the letter fiercely in his hand, but he did not look at her.

Instead, a fierce ejaculation, that came strangely from the lips of the kindly squire, broke from him, and he averted his face, looking out of the window. A moment they sat thus, the beams of the sunset stealing in upon them and touching the girl's jetty hair with a golden gleam. Then Dora's hand stole back again into the trembling hand of the good squire. " Papa," she whispered, her soul in her face, g, papa, is it true V There was a moment of keen suspense—a moment of bitterest, sharpest agony—of breathless waiting —and then the squire answered, huskily: "Yes, Dora; the letter is true." A quick, sharp cry broke from the girl's lips. All day she had struggled against her convictions of Mrs. Narr's veracity ; but now every vestige of hope was destroyed. "Oh, papa !" she cried, with a burst of uncon- trollable sobs. "Oh, father !" The squire put out his shaking arm and drew her to his brest. "It is true, Dora," he repeated, "but I never meant you to kndw it. I thought I could keep it from you always. You are as dear to me as if you were of my own blood ; dearer even than my son. My wife loved you with a mether'f, luve. Yn huve been the light of cm; tho se cotet drop in the cup of my e:Ni,t,l•t!". Is it needful that I should tell you this, dam ii?" "Don't light up, Dora," he said, w:th a strange and new gentleness; "but play me someing. Sing one of the songs I love best." Dora went to the phutn and played and sa.ng a tender old ballad that had been in vogue when t squire was young. She followed it by another and another, and an hour slippod away. She then closed the instrument and went to the squire, with a vague fear tugging at her heart- strings. " Do you feel quite well, papa ? " she asked. "Quite well, darling. You had better go to bed now. It must be after ten. You have been a bless- ing to me, my daughter. You have been the sun- shine of my life. Heaven bless you now and always as I do. Now, good-night, Dora." rim spoke softly, and -kissed her with a strange solemnly. "I'll make your portion all right to-morrow," he

whispered. - "Are you going to bed now, papa?" "Pretty soon. I want to enjoy the quiet peace and pleasantness of this hour. Go, Dora." He put her gently from him. The girl went out from his presence with a weight at her heart greater even than it had been on his return. She went up to her room, but not to bed. There was something unquieting in the very air. She wrote in her journal, balanced her housekeeping accounts, and then sat down by her window, listening for his step on the stairs. An hour passed, and he did not come. Another hour, and the clock in the stable-yard rang out the hour of twelve.

Frightened, Dora stole out into the hall and listened.

"He must have fallen asleep down there!" she said. "The night air will hurt him!" She listened yet more intently, and still heard no sound. Then she glided down the stairs like a spirit, and entered the sitting-room. The squire still sat in the high-backed chair by the window, with the soft night air blowing in upon him. Papa !" cried Dora, softly. He did not answer. For the first time in the girl's life that kindly voice did not reply to her call.

A vague terror convulsed Dora's heart. " Papa ! " she cried, her voice ringing sharply through the room like a bugle call.

Ana ntiii he did not answer.

Dora flew to him, but the form she clasped was cold and rigid. There was a smile on the still face —a smile frozen there. The lifeless, pulseless hands lay on his knees like hands of wax. The generous heart had ceased its pulsations. The tate of the Chessoms had stolen upon the noble old squire in a delicious peace and stillness; and Dora's best friend, her shield against soirow, her bulwark against trouble, was dead !

CHAPTER VIII. THE COUSINS.

On the return of Felix Warner to Horsham' after his visit to Chessom Grange' as described, he found awaiting him the message from Lord Champney, sent to him at his chambers in London, and forwarded to him by his valet. To describe the emotions with which he read the telegram would be a difficult task. Rage and fear predominated. He has gone to Saltair—the love-sick fool !" he muttered, hoarsely. "Was it for this I have kept by his side all these years, making myself his drudge and secretary? Was it for this I have toiled and planned? Why, at any time since he left her, Champney, proud as he was and is, would have come back and humbled himself to his wife, but that I artfully turned his relenting thoughts each time into sentiments of pride and anger, and made him ashamed of his weakness. Gone to Saltair ! I wonder if his proud wife took him back. I wonder if she has overlooked the past, and they are lovers again. Oh, I could kill them both—the fools

He paced his inn chamber with furious steps. His soul was black with passion. "I can never bear it he said to himself. "He calls me to coma to him, and I will go! If they have made up," and he smiled darkly, "they can quarrel again. Both proud, both passionate—one cold as an iceberg, the other jealous as Othello—it will be easy to effect their separation again. A man couldn't ask better materials than I shall have to work upon. I must be on hand at once, as wise and vigilant as a serpent !" He hastened to pack his valise, and to prepare for departure. "I'll write a note to Dora' telling her that my cousin has summoned me," he thought. "The little girl likes me as well as I like her, and I am am sure of her whenever I choose to come back after her. Not a bad speculation to secure the little heiress who has as good blood as any in the country, if the squire isn't noble! attend to business first, then lovely Dora and pleasure !"

He sat down at his table and wrote a note to Dora, giving her his address at Norfolk, and asking her to write to him.

Then, taking the letter and his valise, he hurried down stairs to the tap-room. As he knew a train was nearly due for London, he hurriedly settled his bill, ordered his letter to be posted, and hastened to the station. He was just in time for the up-express, booked himself for a seat in a first-class compartment, and was soon whirling over the rails on his journey. It was early afternoon when he reached London. Ascertaining that there was a connection by the Eastern Counties Railway, he took a cab and pro- ceeded to the station, instead of going to his chambers.

- He was fortunate in making the connection, and was soon again proceeding swiftly on his way. It was late when he arrived at Norwich, and he drove to a hotel, where he quartered himself for the night. It may be imagined that he slept little, and that his thoughts, while awake, were not pleasant. They roved from Dora whom he loved to the Lady Barbara where he hated, not because she had wronged him, but because, as he expressed it, she stood in his way. He heaped imprecations upon the head of Lord Champney, cursed his own folly in having left his lordship to himself so much of late, and murmured: set matters straight directly, or go to an asylum for imbeciles. To undo the mischief Champ- ney has wrought in coming here, shall be my first work. Then m ry my little Dora, and go into society. Won't the little wild-rose create a sensa- tion ?" He dwelt fondly upon the remembrance of Dora's beauty and goodness. If there was a pure spot in his foul heart, Dora had taken possession of it. He loved to think of her stainless purity and guile- less innocence. No haughty beauty or belle could ever have won his heart. Bad as he was in soul, he had always cherished an ideal which he had never found realised until he met Dara. He fell asleep at last, thinking of her, who at that moment was standing beside the dead squire. He wakened early, descended to his breakfast, and then ordered a carriage to convey him the re- mainder of his journey. In a litrle while thereafter he was on his way to the Saltair Manor. It was about noon when the vehicle paused before the great brazen gates of the Manor, and a boy ran out of the lodge swinging the key in his hand. Ihe gates were unlocked and thrown open, and the carriage rolled through, going slowly up the avenue. - _

As on the previous day, at the hour of Lord Champney's arrival, there were groups of people leaning against the balustrade on the cliff, over looking the sea, and other groups clustered under the trees.

"Got company, eh ?" muttered Warner, sav- agely, under his breath. " Invited guests to wit- ness their reconciliation, perhaps. No doubt I shall find my interesting couple in a state of blissful folly !" •

There was no trace of bitterness in his counte- nance, however, as he alighted at the porch and disin,ssed his carriage. To the contrary, ho was as bland and smiling as if the occasion were the happiest in his life. A servant met him at the door and ushered him up to his room at once. A few minutes later he was joined there by Lord Champney. His lordship was not the picture of a happy lover, or a reconciled husband. His face was gloomy, his manner moody and restrained. Warner's heart grew lighter at s4,ht of him, reading in his appearance an augury favourable to himself.

The false friend rushed forward, greeting the baron ‘vith effusion.

"I was never so amazed in my life, Champney," he exclaimed, wringing his lordship's hand, "as when I received your telegram, which came to me this morning. I was down in Sussex, but made all haste to come and Congratulate you on your restored happiness. You at Saltair ! You happy at last in the possession of domestic bliss ! With my heart, I congratulate you." Warner's acting was perfect. Lord Champney was touched by it. LOOKING UP A HOSPITAL FAILURE.

I as amused, writes the reporter of a London paper, when instructed by my editor to interview a person. whose experiences I am about to relate,at.the injunction not to exaggerate the case in any way. As a rule a reporter is expected to make the most of a piece of nevvs. However, I didn't exaggerate.

The individual I was asked to interview is Mr. George Sharpe, of 6, Essex-terrace, Town-road, Lower Edmon- ton. I found him to be a respectable young workman, of an intelligent countenance, with a bright eye, and a healthy fresh colour on his cheeks. In reply to inquiries, however, he said he had alwaY's until recently suffered bad health—coughimg and spitting blood. He had been to the City-road Chest Hospital about two years ago, and they wanted to keep him in there. He was pouiticed and given physic and embrocation for the chect, and directed to feed himself up on cod liver oil, which, he said, he did until it made him sick. Later, he went to the Cavendish-square Hospital. They attributed his illness to the dust of his trade getting into his lutgs. He was under treatment about eighteen months,without relief- continuing all the while to cough and spit blood. Be could not sleep at night,and was as tired when he got uP in the morning as when he went to bed. The patient'S mother said she had had a large family,several of what had died from consumption. "George," she added,"wag just the same until lately _ but now he is wonderfully' changed: he can eat anything at every meal since has been taking these pilla."

" What pills P" asked the reporter, in surprise. " What pills ? Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People," was the reply. "Itis to them that he Oscel his life, and recovery of health, for nothing else did 13111 the least good." Mr. Sharpe said, " One morning when I went t: work my fellow-worken were speaking about rn!" d saying how weak I was. One of them said he believe Dr. Williams' Pink Pills were very good things n andlilen; tioned some cases that had bee cured by them. So got some,taking them after meals. In a short time the blood-spitting, night sweats, and weakness stopped. I feel quite right now, and can stop at my work and eat well."

This is not by any means the first ,:ase in which incipient consumption has been cured in the same way. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills are world-famed for repairing ravages of all debilitating disease and of over work. They cure all the many disorders which arise from an impoverished state of the blood and nerves, such ae anaemia, pale and sallow complexion, general muocular weakness, loss of appetite, shortness of breath, pains in the back, nervous headache, early decay, all forms 01 female weakness, hysteria, pAralysis, lccomotor ataxy', sciatica, palpitations,low vitality, and wasting strength from any cause. These Pills are genuine only with the full name, Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People, and are sold by chemists and by Dr. Williams' Medicine Company, 4d, Holborn-viaduct, London, E.G., at 29. 9d. a box, or six for 13s. 91. They are unrivalled as tonic and strengthening medicine for both sexes. Pint pills sold loose or from glass jars are not genuine; do no; accept them.

LADIES' LETTER.

Ky DZAR Ikhazuc, There is nothing very new to be noted in head- gear just now. Bright red straw hats, trimmed, of course, with black and cream only, will be worn very soon, and they really look much nicer than they sound. Big felt and velvet picture hats will certainly be in favour once again, and toques will be more fantastic than ever: but more of these anon. I must tell you, however, that the newest bows for hats are of white glace silk, edged with black velvet.

Black and white is always an effective and dis. tinguished combination, and its good p oints are exemplified in a hat of coarse white, English band-made straw, the front being occupi, 3d by two cloudy rosettes of white lisse, backed by a bow of black lisse and a high, black osprey,,, while two white wings shine out against the crow ,n, which is surrounded at the base by soft puffmgs of black lisse.

Coat and skirt costumes are really the only wear just now; in fact, the tailor-built -woman is hav- ing things all her own way, and. quite right too, for in the country, whether on motr or mountain, or alewhere elee, nothing looks sn3arter, or is !awe

comfortable than s well-made gown, plain without any frippery to get torn or untidy, the skirt not too full, clearing the ground frankly, and worn over a well-fitting knicker-petticoat. A smart gown of drab beaver cloth has the straight double- breasted coat fastened with white pearl buttons, and having a 'little silver braiding on the high military collar. _ _

A coritume forthe moors and for general tourist wear might be made of covert coating in soft dark clover-green; the double-breasted coat is lined through with striped satin, and fastened with big smoke-pearl buttons, and has a deep collar of velvet.

Real novalt, in the world of costume there is not, so I think I shall discuss at the moment the possibility of converting our old frocks into petti- coats. I stall take, for instance, a black brocaded tea-gown, gay with little groups of coloured flowers; proceed to induce it mentally to form a pale skirt with a very deep flounce of pale pink glare silk, trimmed on the hem and on the top with a thickly quilled ruche, and lightly covered from beneath the upper ruche with a flounce of black Belk-edged chiffon. Black lace might take the place of the chiffon for those who prefer it. And the same petticoat would lend itself to the more expensive decoration of black muslin flounces with insertions of cream-coloured Valenciennes lace and nanow bands of blue baby ribbon. A plain satin petticoat may be very successfully trimmed with a broad row of black velvet ribbon on the extreme hem, above which should be sewn thirteen rows of narrow black baby ribbon—and here is occupation for the leisure moments of the industrious. Then, again, a petticoat of worthy ,detail may be made of an old black satin dress with a deeply gathered flounce trimmed with gold galoon—one, two or three rows of this to be used according to the pocket of the proposed wearer. The best lining for satin petticoats is nun's veiling, failing of course am only suggesting the nun's veiling for tie advantage of the prudent, but as a rule, if we urot an old dress to make a petti- coat, the old silkeia lining may be relied upon to do its duty once agaits. I do not believe the woman lives who has not, in her heart of hearts, a sneaking fancy for dainty hose, and there ace some really lovely stockings about just now. ror wearing with tourist and moor costumes, there are stockings of the finest wool, woven in tweed designs to match the dresses. For ordinary occasions, fine black cashmere, lightly embroidered up the front with white or grey, are the nicest; those with coloured stitching and open- work are not good form for street wear now, and are reserved for tlx9 house, and the same may bs said of the chic striped and checked hose in span silk or lisle thread.

Your loving JENNIN

"WOMEN'S CHAT."

The Duke and Duchess of York's little sons, who have I been spending some time with their grandmother, the I Queen, together with Princess Henry of Battenberg's children make quitee nursery full. Her Majesty is in- tensely fond of children and never kappier than with the little people around her. In most respects the nur- sery arrangoments and the general principles pursued,in the case o, the Queen's grandchildren and great grand- children are identical with those which obtained in the early years of her Majesty's own married life, when her nursery was full cf small princes and princesses, several of whom are now themselves grandparents! The oldest Queen in Europe, the Queen of Denmark, obtained her seventy-ninth birthday on Tuesday last. Her Majesty is a Princess of Hessel-Cassel and married the present King of Denmark,who is her senior by a few ,months,in 1842. As the zustom of sending birthday pre- sents is studiously kept up by her children and grand- children, the number of paroels which arrived at the Palace were considerable. Lieutenant Dan Godfrey who has just reached the age of sixty-five, and whose retirement ender the age rule of the service will shortly take place,has had charge of the Grenadier Guards Band for over forty years. He, I was appointed in July 1858 by the late Prince Consort The Lieutenant has, however, a scheme for starting a band of his own about the end of the year,which will no doubt receive the warm support of his many admirers. It is distinctly a matter for regret that the old- fashioned " harvest home." festivities in rural districts are getting less and less every year. Time was when every farmer would gather his men and their families together in his biggest barn at the end of the harvest, and celebrate the fruition of the years' work with an evening of festivity and merriment, but now a change seems to have come over the relation between farmer and labourer. The rural labourer is rapidly getting into the position of the town workman, the old personal relationship between master and servant is dying out, and the "cash nexus" is the only tie between them nowadays. "But, if the "harvest home" is dying out,we still,hap- pily, have the harvest thanksgiving with us. Last Sun- day,and for a Sunday or two to come, the great majo- rity of our churches were, and will be decked out, and there are few prettier sights than a village church with its decorations of flowers, fruit, and corn, on a harvest Sunday. That this custom will sooner or later go the way of the ether is, also, only too likely, but all who value the human as well as the commercial aspect of rural life will hope that it may only follow at a distance that is still far off. _ _

Many and spirited have been the discussions going on in some of the London papers of late, as to whether or P 0 it is wrong to instruct our servants to say" Not at Home," when we do not desire to receive callers. Surely there can be no possible harm in this polite, and recog- nised method of denying oneself. But it is the dull season and in all probability when this particular topic has been worn threadbare some other exciting subject will receive attention. By the way, the custom of having days "At Home "is by no means new. It was prevalent in Queen Ann's tune when women were "at home once a week to their friends of both sexes and called it a " day "just as we do. Across the channel, a new form of amusement has sprang up. A number of persons secure small toy balloons,and repair to a large open space free from tree. Postcards are then attached to the balloons, and at a signal each is released by the owner. The individual who sees the balloon fall is requested to note, upon the card, the hour and place, and then post it. At the end of eight days the answers to hand are examined and the prizes awarded to the happy competitor whose balloon accomplished the greatest number of miles ere descending to earth again. Speaking of balloons, many of my readers may be interested to know, that a storm viewed from above the clouds, has the appearance of ebullition. The upper surface of the cloud is bulged upward and outward, and has the resembl ince of a vast sea of boiling upheaving snow. The falling of the rain can be distinctly heard, making a noise like a water- fall over a preeipice, but thunder heard above a storm cloud is not loud, and the flashes of lightening appear like streaks of intensity white light. Only two Royal ladies have, as yet, been up in a balloon, the Queen Christina of Sweden and the ex-Empress Eugene. Boot-woven stockings, which have precisely the appearance of cloth gaiters, without the increased large- ness of size which these useful articles give, will enjoy considerable favour this coining winter, among up-to- date women who indulge in much out-door exercise. They are made in brown tan, arhi black, and are even being adorned with tiny buttons to still further carry out the illusion. Princess Maud's trousseau included several pairs. The reefer coat is once again with us I notice, and where a covering has perforce to do duty for two or three winters there is no safer style to select. Many of the long mantles now being shewn for matrons recall the gorgeous habits worn by the Flemish nobles and burghers that Holbein has perpetuated. The richest brocaded velves with bald large patterns are employed for them, and the collars stand up very high in the neck. They can be tinned flown, however, and probably often will be.

No self-respecting neck is now seen without a ruffling of some sort, but as the winter advances, simple tuckers of white lace are to be correct wear. This is welcome news, for the hard line of ribbon drawn high and tight round the threat, a mode we long indulged in, was only suitable to the very few, and the ruffles which followed proved far from becoming to the majority of us. A soft touch of white lace, however, is ever a delightful and becoming finish to our necks, and we shall do well to embrace the mode. The newest idea in stationery is to have the corner of the sheet of paper turned down, and held in place by a coloured seal or wafer, which displays the Christian name or monogram. This fancy is in high favour for wedding invitations. The seal is in silver, but not the lettering in which the guests are bidden—that is a little out-of-date. Probably there never were so many short sighted people in the world as there are at the present time,and there is no doubt that this is in consequence of the strain to which the eyes are subjected, and to the lack of fresh air consequent on a sedendary occupation. A simple proof of this is supplied by the fact that sailors and savages who spend their time in the open air,, never suffer from short or weak sight. A famous oculist has recently pronounced his opinion that a systematic method of resting the eyes is of incalculable benefit to those who have to use them for close work. If e advises the worker to pause and look round the room once, at least, in every ten minutes. By doing this, the muscular tension is relieved, the eyes are rested, and the blood supply better. One of the best methods of cleansing a sponge is to piece it in a basin of warm water containing a large handful of salt. The soap can be removed by this means without injury to the sponge. By the way, a certain number of our sponges are obtained off the Coast of Tripoli, and at present there are about 1,700 men en- gaged in the sponge fishery there. To keep a wateh in good order, which our mankind are fond of saying, is an impossible feat on our part, the same temperature should be preserved, if possible, at all times, likewise the same position. Winding should be peziormed in the morning always, as by this means the strongest tension on the sprin4 is preserved during the period most liable to rough usage. The average watch is made up of 175 different pieces, comprising upwards of 2,400 separate and distinct operations in its manufac- ture.

The skins and cores of apples should never be thrown away, as an excellent jelly may •be made by placing them in a preserving pan with a few cloves and water sufficient to cover them. Simmer till quite soft, strain through a jelly bag and to every pint of juice allow a pound of sugar. Boil until it stiffens, a few drops of cochineal will improve the color. .

" ATADGE."

MARRGIAE OF MR_ _III, E.__ RANSOME,

OF WARRINGTON.

An interesting wedding took place at the Friends' Meeting House, Mount-st,, Manchester, at eleven oclock on Wadnesday morning last in the presence of a number of invited relatives and friends, when the contracting parties were Mr. Harold E. Ransome,of 14, Museum-st., Warrington, the respected manager of Messrs. Unsworth and Richmond's works, Academy-st., and Miss Alice Jane Oakes, daughter oi Mr. William Oakes, of Macclesfield. The cere- mony was a brief but very impressive one. After meditation and prayer, the two contracting parties made a simple declaration, and afterwards signed the marriage register in the presence of Mr. Bing, the registrar of the Friends Society. Their signa- tures were afterwards attested by some forty or fifty friends. The bride looked very handsome, and was attired in a dress of cream mohair, trimmed with corded silk and pearls. She wore a picture hat trimmed with cream satin ribbon and cream plumes, and carried a beautiful bouquet. There were no bridesmaids in attendance. The duties of best man were satisfactorily fulfilled by Mr. John A. Ransome. After the ceremony, the party was photographed by Mr. T. Birtles, of Warrington. Subsequently a reception was held in the drawing- room of the Friend's Institute, adjoining the meet- ing house, and the guests then sat down to a "breakfast." The health of the bride and bride- groom was proposed in very suitable language by Mr. Ransome (father of the bridegroom) and was fittingly responded to. Amongst the guests Present were—Mr. and Mrs. Oakes (Macclesfield), Mr. E. R. Ransome, and the Misses Ransome (London),Mr. and Mrs. F. B. Oakes and Miss Alice Oakes, Mr. Arthur J. Ransome, Mr. S. J. Ransome, Mr. and Mrs. Richmond, and Master Frank and Miss Bertha Richmond, Mr. and Mrs. Adshead (Macclesfield), Mr. and Mrs. F. Adshead, Miss Adshead, Mr. and Mts. William Harrison, Mr. George Harrison, and Mr. H. Harrison, Miss Har- rison, Miss Gertrude Harrison, Mr. and Mrs. T. Wadsworth; Mr. Thomas Wadsworth, Mrs. Hovis Smith, Miss Atherton, Mrs. G. F. Gleniay, Mrs. Mur- gatroyd, Miss Fitton, Mr. Montague Atkins, Miss Wallis (London), and Miss Potts (Macclesfield). The newly-married couple departed amid the well- wishes of their friends for a tour in Scotland. A large number of presents were received, the one from the works of Messrs. Unsworth and Richmond being a piano. WARRINGTON ASSOCIATION OF BANDS.

THE BENEFIT PERFORMANCE.

On Saturday evening a special meeting of this Asso elation was held in the Council Chamber at the Town Hall, when the principal business was to make a pre- sentation to Mr. Gilbert Lowe, a former member of the Volunteer band and also Mr. Down's band, who met with a serious accident a short time ago whilst workiag at Messrs. Greenings works, and resulting in the loss of his right arm. Owing to a misunderstanding Mr. Lowe was not present, and it was decided that the cheque should be handed to Mr. Joseph Taylor, hon. secretary of the Association, for presentation to Mr. Lowe. rhe proceedings were presided over by Mr. T. J. Down.— The Secretary stated that up to the present time 49 per- formences had been given in the Park, at a cost of £44 163. 6d. When the season was concluded 54- per- formances would have been given, including the massed band performance on October 3rd.—The Chairman : What will they have cost the town ?—Mr. Taylor: £5O. —The Chairman: Not au extravagant price.—Mr. Tay- lor observed that up to the end of September they would have spent £49 12s. 6i out of the £so.—The Chair- man: That is running it very fine. (Laughter.)— Mr. Taylor remarked that Warrington was the only place in which performances were given in the Park in September. The season commenced earlier and lasted longer. He wished to state that he had heard some good news, namely, that it was the intention of the Town Hall, Park and Baths Committee to illuminate the band-stand next season, when requisite.—Mr. John Fairhurst (borough treasurer) said he did not think the the matter had been before the committee. The state- ment was not an official one.—The Chairman said he had made a suggestions to that effect himself to someone. At times it had been too dark to read the music.—Mr. Gor- ton: On one occasion did it not become so dark that the band could not see to play" God save the Queen "? (Laughter.)—Mr. Fairhurst : It would not be the first hand I have heard of as being similarly -bred. (Laugh- ter.—The subject then dropped.--The Chairman re- marked that in some cases the band secretary did not apply for the money to which the band was entitled for playing in the Park until long afteiwards,and that made it awkward for Mr. Fairh urst, who had to keep the accounts correctly. He did not know what the available surplus was in consequenee of the outstanding aeceunts. It would be better if some time was fixed in which money must be claimed or forfeited. (Hear, hear.)—Mr. Dell moved that the period be 21 days.—This was seconded by Mr. Gorton, and carried unanimously.—The Chair- man said Mr. Lowe's absence made his position a rather aifficult one. He had hoped he would have been present, so that he might have had an opportunity of congratu- lating him upon his marvellous recovery from the terrible injuries he received a short time ago. It seemed to him that Mr. Lowe had added a little testimony to the useful- ness and value of that admirable institution the War- rington Infirmary—(hear, hear)—and also to the surgical skill of the doctors and the excellent staff. If the man had been conveyed in his critical condition to Manches- ter or Liverpool. as used to be the case when a serious accident occurred, it was more than probable that death would have taken place, but thank!' to the immediate attention he was able to receive, and to his own pluck and sound constitution, he had been restored to a good state of health and strength. Unfortunately, however, he had lost Ins right arm, but he was able to do without it. A week or two ago lie (the speaker) was going down Horsemarket-st. when he saw Lowe riding on a bicycle. That was not altogether an easy task for a one-armed man. He had also learnt to play his in- strument with his left hand,and he seemed to be able to play as well as usual. (Flear,hear.) When the news of the accident became known it touched to the heart every bandsman in the town, and all who were con- nected with music felt the deepest sorrow and sym- pathy for him. It was, therefore, with feelings of the most profound relief that they heard he was likely to get over the accident. A fund was started with the object of raising sufficient money to enable Lowe to start some business within his power. The cAll was most cheerfully responded to, and the respectable sum of £2l is. 10d. was raised, principally amoagst the members of the various bands, mostly workingmen who had not much to spare. They were prepared to hand £2l ls.lod. over to himetnd they hoped it would be of some service to him in making a fresh start in life. He was sorry Mr. Lowe was absent,but although that was the case he was sure they would all join with him in wishing him I prosperity and success in his future life. (Applause.) He would ask Mr. Taylor to hand him a cheque for the amount named, and also to express the good feelings of the meeting. It was possible further sums might yet come in, and they would be handed to Mr. Taylor by Mr. Fairhurst, and Mr. Taylor would pass them on to Mr. Lowe. (Applause.)—Mr. Taylor explained that a special performance was given by the Volunteer Band in the Park in aid of the testimonial fund. I Mr. Taylor remarked that the members of the bands I which played in the park did not receive money indivi- dually. It was handed over to the vat ions treasurers, and was spent in the purchase of music and in the re- pair of instruments. (Hear, hear.) It was all merged into a common band fund.—The Chairman remarked that that was very praiseworthy on the part of the bands. He had been under the impression that they put the money into their own pockets.—A hearty vote of thanks having been accorded to Mr. Down for presiding the proceedings terminated with a vote of thanks to the Borough Treasurer for his services.

RECOGNITION MEETING.

Once again the Wesleyans of Warrington assembled on Monday evening to welcome in their midst the new ministers who have been appointed by the Conference to this circuit. The recognition meeting was held in the Wesleyan schoolroom, Bold-street, and was largely attended, representatives being present from mest of the Wesleyan Chapels in the circuit. To schoolroom had been tastefully decorated for the occasion and presented an attractive appearance. Mr. William Bennett, of Padgate, presided, and he was supported on the platform by the two new ministers : Rev. W. Potts (superintend- ent), and the Rev. G. Harbottle; also by the Rev. W. G. Triggs, Rev. J. Gunnell (Penketla), Mr. J. Summersg,ill and Mr. L. G. Hensball. After devotional exercises, the CHAIRMAN in a few introductory remarks said they ought to hope for and pray for the prosperity of the work of God in the Cir- cuit. If they were to have this prosperity they must be united and not depend too much upon their ministers. He had not the least doubt but that their ministers would do their part, but they as congregations must also do their part. He was very glad to see the Revs. W. Potts and G'. Harbottle present, but he was sorry their wives were unable to be there. Mrs. Potts was unwell and Mrs. Harbottle was in the same condition. He was, however, glad to see Miss Potts, and he was sure she would receive a hearty welconae. (Applause.) Mr lizetsneee said he was very pleased to join in the expression of welcome to their new ministers. He agreed with meetings of that kind because he felt that if their church was to be prosperous they must have sympathy between the pulpit and the pew. (Hear, hear.) They must welcome their ministers amongst them and let them think that they had their sympathy, and see that they were going to stand by them and sup- port them in the circuit. (Applause.) He did not think the circuit was ever better disposed to see or more anxious to welcome ministers amongst them than they were that day. He trusted that they would be deter- mined as a church and as individuals to consecrate themselves afresh to the Master's service, and then with united earnest ministry working harmoniously together, and the church prepared to back them up, be was sure they would enter upon another prosperous and success- ful period. He would like to say a word about their cir- cuit finances. Most of them were aware that their finances had not been in a healthy state for some little time. He enjoined all to do their best to relieve them of the difficulty. They ought to make it more a matter of conscience than they did. He was sure if they did so their financial troubles would disappear. He trusted they were going to enter on a still more prosperous career and that they would support the cause by their presence and prayers to the utmost of their powers. Mr. SIIMMERSGILL in a word of welcome on bebalf of the local preachers in the circuit said there were at present thirty local preachers on the plan. There were sixteen preaching places in the circuit,and they had four ministers,so that there were twelve preaching places left for,the local preachers to fill Sunday after Sunday. (Hear, hear.) He mentioned that in order to call attention to the fact that the localipreachers were of some use in the carrying on of circuit work. (Applause.) They repre- sented an important portion of the circuit, and in addi- tion to being local preachers some were cless leaders, some stewards, and others connected with Sunday school and other Christian work, so that they claimed some interest at their hands. He could say to the new ministers that they were a loyal people to the Methodist doctrine and the Methodist discipline. They were one in sympathy one in purpose, and one in earnestness in a desire to give Mr. Potts and Mr.Harbottle a most hearty welcome into that circuit. (Applause.) He hoped that both of them would have no cause to regret coming into that circuit through want of co-operation on the part of the local preachers. Ho believed they desired to unite and do what they could for the spread of Christianity in that circuit. (Applause.) Mr. GEORGE WEAVER speaking on behalf of the Bold- st. Society offered to the new ministers a right hearty welcome. He thought that at Bold-st.they were loyal to their ministers, and he hoped they would ever be found working in accord with the ministers for the benefit of the church. (Applause.) Councillor SHAW GREEN as representihg Bewsey-road chapel said he believed the congregation at Rewsey-road would offer to their new ministers everything they ought to and could do,in order to make their stay in War- rington a blessing to them and a success in the cause of God. When Mr. Watkins left them he said that he was proud to go out of the circuit leaving Bewsey-read a united, contented, and happy people. That was so, and he (Councillor Shaw Green) was glad to be able to say that there was co-operation in all sections of the work for the spread of their religion. (Applause.) He thought the new ministers would find that the heartiness of Lan- cashire men was to be found at Bewsey-road. With re- gard to the remarks respecting local preachers they at Bewsey-road wanted them to feel thatthey were preach- ing to people as ready to take their message as that from their ministers. (Applause.) He hoped they would all endeavour to make their ministers stay amongst them a great and happ? success. (Applause.) The Rev. W. Poxes on rising to address the meeting met with a hearty reception. He said he was very glad to see that large gathering of friends, and he was sorry that his wife was not also present. Unfortunately she took a cold during the removal /and had been in bed the best part of that and the previous day, but he hoped she would soon be in their midst. He did not know whether they called Warrington a beautiful town, but it had not given them a brilliant reception so far in it- self. He was very glad the sunshine was coming out and he hoped there would be a great deal of sunshine in their intercourse and work with each other. In his study he found a copy of their local Church Record for this month, and very unsuspiciously he thought he should get to know something of Warrington, but the first thing he saw Was a portrait of himself. (Laugh- ter.) He did not know how the sketch of his career came to be written, but he was very much obliged for the good things said about it and he only hoped that he should be able to live up io it. (Applause.) He was very glad to be in the midst of a community composed chiefly of men of his own county—for he was a Lancashire man. He found that Lancashire people had good conceit of themselves (laughter)—but he had found grad people out of Lanca- shire. There were many things in the experience of a Methodist preacher which were not pleasant and they felt them very much at these times. It was very hard to break off friendships with people amongst whom they had been labouring but they were always very pleased to feel that they had people who would be glad to see them and who would give them a hearty welcome in what ever circuit they went to. (Applause.) Lancashire people had their faults there was no doubt,but they had also their excellencies. Straightforwardness was one of them, and he (Mr. Potts) always tried. to be straight- forward. His last Lancashire circuit was - at Oldham, and although the people might be rough—they were called Oldham " roughyeds"— he found them very straight. (Applause.) They had been reminded that evening that success did net depend upon the ministers alone, and there was one good thing in connection with itinerancy and that was when a new minister came amongst them they received him with hopefulness. Still the proslperity of the work depended upon them as well as the ministers. It would be a groat mistake if there was any want of sympathy between the travelling preachers, and the local :preachers, and the man who was a ;local preacher stood in the Pulpit on the same level as he did. (Applause.) He wished them to understand that they were not mere stopgaps, and he wanted them to take heart and hope, and go to their work with that same confidence which he and his colleagues would go to theirs. He hoped they would encourage each other in the great work Co which they had been call. He also hoped that the Sunday school teachers would realise the immense importance of the work in which they were engaged. He thought that work was very much under- estimated. (Hear, hear.) He was of the opinion that the work of the Sunday school teacher was even a more important work than that of the preacher of the gospel. Proceeding to speak of the work of visitation he ex- pressed the hope Chat any who thought themselves being neglected in this matter should speak to them. He did not believe in finding fault with ministers behind their backs. Mr. Harbottle would take the pastoral visitation connected with Bewsey-road, and Mr. Triggs would have that in connection with Bold-street. In con- clusion he referred to the Sunday services,and remarked that he did not take" half-timers." (Laughter.) Be was not going to argue that very important question now,but they must remember that their presenee in the House of God was an element of strength. Let them give themselves to Ged's grace, and in everything they did,mieht God he with them and in them. (Auplauee.) The Rev. G. EIARBOTTLE said he had been trying to recollect the time when he attended a welcome meeting before, and it carried him back twelve years of his life when in Cardiff. He hoped they had now recognised him and that if they happened to meet him in the streets they would recognise him there. (Applause.) If he passed any of them in the streets it would be their fatelt if they did not stop him. He would not like to pass even a chimney-sweep amongst them without shaking his hand. (Applause.) He had come to Warrington to be at home. He could say that they at Warrington had a very good character from those ministers who had laboured amongst them. He knew a good many of them. Mr. Freeman—(applause)—had been his near neighbour, and Mr. E. H. Jackson—(applauee)—wae also a friend of his as well as his former superintendent —Mr. Gunnell. (Applause.) Hearing such a good report of Warrington he decided to accept their invite- tion,and he wanted to believe that the best spot was that upon which he stood. (Applause.) He had als ) learned the lesson that the best moments were the present moments. He was glad to think that he should occupy Beweey-road pulpit oftee, because he liked preaching to his congregation more than six times a quarter. It was not so much the itinerancy as the itinerancy within the circuit, which he disliked. If a minister only occupied his pulpit three Sundaye in a quarter what chance had he of doing much good? He hoped that he should have to preach more than six ser- mons a quarter to his congregation at Bewsey-road. He had never left any place but with deep regret, and he

always felt that any place where he lived was his home. Be looked upon life as a great opportunity and his desire was to serve God. (Applause.) The speeches were agreeably interspersed with vocal and instrumental selections. Solos were exceedingly well interpreted by Miss Hannah Brough, and Mr. Levi Richardspn, and Miss J. C. WARRINGTON TECHNICIL INSTITUTE.

The list of classes for the session 1896-7 has just been prepared, and the arrangements are on similar lines to those which obtained last year. The Continuation School will be under the superintendency of Mr.R.J.Half at People's College, Arpley-st. The school will open three evenings weekly, commencing Monday, 21st Sept. the fees are 2s. 61. a term (14 weeks) or 31. a week. Students who, havirq..! left school some time, wish to recover their knowledge of the ordinary subjects, will find these claves well suited for their purpose. Separate classes are provided for adult students. 13:‘gintiers generally are strongly recommended to join the Contin- uation School for a session before proceed- ing to the Science or Commercial Classes. Prizes and scholarships, tenable in the higher depart- ments. will be awarded at the end of the session.

The School of Art re-opens on Tuesday, Bth September and evening classes will commence on September 21st. 'rise fees are as follows :—Evening classes, elementary, 103. ; advanced, 153. for the session. There are special terms for el.?mentary teachers. For embroidery and woodcarving, the fee is 51. each per term, students of the school, 24. 6i. The headmaster of the school is Mr. C. B. Aylward, and the subjects taught include drawing in outline and in light and shade, geometry, modelling in clay, principles of ornament, design, painting, figure and life-classes, lectures on historic are, embroidery, woodcarving. _ _

The Szliool of Slience and Commercial Clas•s wilt recommence on Monday, 21st September. In addition to the usual classes in commerical and scientific subjects technical classes in iron and steel, tanning, and dress- making, as well as in sloyd woodwork, and music will be fox med.

Soh@larships, enabling Students to continue their studies at the school in another session are given to all who obtain a first-class in the examinations of the Science and Art Department, the Union of Lancashire and Cheshire Institutes, the Society of Arts, or the City and Guilds of London Institute.

The fees will be as fol lows,—For the session(September to May), each lecture class, ss. ; two related subjects, 74. 61., three, 103. ; practical chemistry,lo4. (aavanced, 12s. 6 1., honours, 159.) ; practical metallurgy, 10s. ; sloyd, 103. ; dressmaking, ss. per term ( day class, 10s.) Inclusive Fees are arranged for Science and Art subjects aken together.

Mr. Jacob, the well-knswn curiosity momer of Simla, who is supposed to be the original of Marion Crawtord's " Mr. Isaacs," is dispersing his famous collection, and his mart will soon be a thing of the past. Owing to the aetion of the Indian Government since the Hyderabad diamond case, it is virtually impossible for him to carry on business any longer with the Maharajahs of Native States, who have hitherto been his principal patrons, so he has resolved to retire upon his laurels. A gentleman who was recently shown over Mr. Jacobs private apart- ments states that the description contained in Mr. Craw- ford's book still fits the the drawing-room exactly—" It seemed to me that I was suddenly transported into the subterranean chamber whither the wicked magistrate sent Aladdin in quest of the lamp. A soft but strong light filled the room, though I did not immediately com- prehend whence it came ; nor did I think to look, so amazed was Iby the extraordinary splendour of the object that met my eyes. In the first glance it appeared as if the walls and ceilings were lined with gold and precious stones, and in reality it was almost literally the truth."
