MINER.

BER 12th, 1896.

probable that withil3 ew days the indigne: ;11 has been excitO , irrespective of sect nti .es will make itself hesrut rough the medium ca' rery side there cane If a widespread belief has come. Even tbe lurkish of people arei hing is being gsine.l by delay. The thend veil-meaning moriareb cumstances to secure of his dominions any seen that ABDCI' less than an assassiO vigorous words of P. the instigator of tl/0 )f his Armenian silt; m for believing that few weeks or montbe lerpetrating deeds even ,hich have shocked 61! ile there was a P°56,:; n interference 'nig"; he condition of thing' deal to be said fpr , but there is no sne" a does not show Of esire to improve lel .4) and sword nurabe: ;elms and harnile' ctually endeavouring „ed pie th ose who escsr-d whole of the wretelle ist• any means of subs r is really a scheme f°o pestilence of tilt° the clubs and w There is another ras A this country sod Pot sed countries ce.Ofe, Lrdered people hsets latenians and sabiletii ;. But the fa°tikeel elusively concentrav of stians. Christians jeci malities are regs6l many well-409°v-

tithoritios predict that an attempt at a general kltssacre of Christians might break out at any mo- eient. The Turkish appetite for blood has been Whetted but it has not yet been satiated. If such 1 Massacre were to be attempted the European' Powers would be bound to interfere, but their in 1 terference would be too late. The Turkish empire Weuld fall to pieces amidst a chaos of bloodshed end the dreaded Eastern question would be re- °Pened in a manner which would make a peaceable settlement almost impracticable. If the singular toleration which the "Concert of Europe" has extended to the Sultan is due to the fear of re- °Pening the Eastern Question, it is about time that the said "Concert" re-considered its posi- tson. It is daily becoming more evident that What is needed in order to prevent the re-open- lag of that perilous question in a very dangerous forra is prompt and well-considered action. It hems now as though nothing short of the deposi- tion of the Sultan and the placing of the Turkish government under some sort of European guaran- tee will boa sufficient remedy for the evils that have ePrung up. The.: sooner some movement in that iirection is made the better. If things are allowed to drift owing to the timidity or mutual jealousies of the Powers until a great crisis doe 3 come, as !Rae it must sooner or later, it will be next to iinpossible to find a peaceful solution of the difficulties. Hence the need for one or Other of the leading European governments to take a firm and resolute course, and there is no government which is better entitled or better able to take such a course than the Government ef this country. Lord SALISBURY has shown y his language on several occasions that he is per- Sonally in favour of dealing in a stringent manner 'With the Sultan, buthitberte his actions have been tauch less vigorous than his words. What is now wanted in this country is some movement to strengthen the Prime Minister's hands and to in- fuse boldness into his foreign policy. The pro- rooters of the agitation that is about to be corn- Menced will certainly not attempt to make any Party capital out of the action they are taking. What they will endeavour to do is to Prove to our own government and to the other governments of Europe that the people of this country are determined not to stand idly and help- lessly by while Christians are being exterminated °4 a wholesale scale by a vicious and fanatical Monarch.

The Runcorn District Council in petitioning the London and North-Wester n Railway Company for reduction in the bridge tolls are moving distinctly With the times. The tendency of the present age le to abolish tolls where possible, and -hile the Council cannot hope to have the bridge -e free It's request to have some reduction made is per- fectly reasonable. The bridge must of necessity llave paid for itself over and over again, and the Ilailway Company might without any serious de pletion of their great income make an appreciable !`eduction in the fare. A tax of is. weekly on the Ilicome of the average workmen for the privilege of tossing the bridge to reach his work is a matter of considerable moment in days when work is scarce kid wages low. If the Council succeeds in this its littest enterprise, the ratepayers will have much to be thankful for.

It may be mentioned that two attempts have baen made to secure a reduction, one in 1884 and the other in 1889. Now in 1896 the Council ask that the fare be reduced to one penny each way and from is. to sixpence per week on the contract The memorial points out the several vital points in the question from a Runcorn point of view and makes out a strong case for the Council. Perhaps the railway company will give their serious con- sideration to the matter and bring about the de- Sired result.

At the meeting of the District Council Dr.Gastaldi again brought the work of the Trades Committee Itto evidence, by asking who ther anything had yet 13een done by that committee. Mr. Speakman, in %ewer, vaguely referred to work being done by in- members of the committee in the private tipacity and, while inclined to generalise ld out some hops of improvement the alleged dullness of trade. It is only a few weeks since we pointed out the helplessness of the committee, and the members of it including kr. Speakman, seem to be realising their delicate Position in dealing with private firms. The com- xaittee as a body can do little or nothing though the personal influence of townsmen both on and off may count for something.

It is a pleasure to see cruelty punished as it was bY the Runcorn magistrates on Monday. Two boat- were proved to have worked two ponies, after repeated warnings,a distance of 27 miles in a condi- tlon which gave the inspector sufficient grounds to tesert that it was a gross act of cruelty. This is by 110 means an isolated case of cruelty to canal boat horses,but no doubt the punishment meted out—to 'William Forster, 103. and cost for working the Ponies:and John Forster 20s. and costs for causing them to be worked—will encourage the officials' of the society to keep a strict look out for similar offenders.

Local magistrates cannot be congratulated on their strict attention to magisterial business at /Itincorn last Monday. At eleven o'clock the usual hour for commencing court work the only justice of the peace present was Mr. A. J. Norman and that Rentleman sat wait;.ng in solitary state for a good half of an hour. Meanwhile, excited policemen ran- Backed the town for a colleague for Mr. Norman, 'while court officials idled the time away and Prisoner's waited in dismal expectancy for the taagistrates who did not come. After considerable Waste of time Mr. Lightburn put in an appearance hnd the work of the court was speedily transacted.

RUNCORN VOLUNTEERS AT HARTFORD.

Through the closing of the shooting range at Runcorn the annual prize meeting of the F. and G. Co.s of the 2nd Earl of Chester's Volunteer Regiment was held on Mon- day at Hartford. The atmospheric conditions were net conducive to high scoring as the weather was very dull, though fine. About 52 competitors took part. Captain G. F. Ashton and the Rev. Dr. Preston were present. RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

TEUNDERSTORIL AT RUNCORN.

A rather severe thunderstorm passed over the Runcorn district on Wednesday aecompanied by vivid flashes of lightning and a heavy fall of rain. No damage is re- ported. THE CLOSING OF THE SHIP CANAL TIDAL OPENINGS.

We underatand that the Ship Canal Company have entered into a contract with Mr. Frank Colson, contrac- tor of Frodaham and Ellesmere Ports,f or closing the tidal openings at Runcorn, the Weaver and Ellesmere Port, and that the work will be commenced at once.

RUNCORN WESLIYAN CIRCUIT.

The Rev. R. J. Clifton, the newly-appointed minister to the Runcorn Wesleyan circuit commenced his duties on Sunday last. He preached in the morning at Halton- road Chapel, and in the evening in St. Paul's Chapel. At both services there were good attendances and the preacher created a favourable impression.

VISIT OF MR. W. H. JUDi TO RUNCORN.

A visit was announced for October 7th of this eminent muaician who will give a musical evening in the Public Hall, Runcorn, on the evening of that date. Mr. Jude's reputation is well known and his recent sacred musical services in Liverpool and the North of England, have drawn large gatherings. Anyone going to hear Mr. Jude's " Musical reminiscences " will not be dis- appointed. RUNCORN SHOP ASSISTANTS V. RUNCORN WHITISTAR. This match was played on the Slop Assistants ground on Wednesday afternoon. Minshull kicked off for the shop men. Just before half time Minshull got the ball from a scrimmage an passed to Nixon who scored a clever try for the horse team. In the second half S. Fearnett of the White Star, fifteen passed to P. Fearnett who managed to get over the line. Neither of the tries were converted so that the game ended in a draw. THE PROPOSED CO-OPERATIVE TOBACCO MANUFACTURY.

The local committee of the Co-operative Stores have received a communication froiri the Wholesale Society. Mr. Brodriek, the secretary, in his letter says, " the committee have this matter under consideration and before any definite action is taken as regards site your letter will be submitted to them and we will communi- cate with you further. It may be some little time before dezided steps are taken." The Runcorn committee are doing everything possible to bring the manufactury to this locality and it is hoped that their efforts will be successful. PRIMITIVE METHODIST CHAPEL, RUNCORN.

The harvest services were held in this chapel 1 ast Sun day when the Rev.W.Fidoe,the newly-appointed pastor, preached three sermons during services held in the morn- ing, afternoon, and evening. The church was appro- priately decorated with fruit,flowers,and vegetables,and the choir contributed special music. Good congregations assembled, and collections were taken on behalf of the circuit fund. On Monday evening a tea party was held which was well attended. RUNCORN SONS OF TEMPERANCE.

BETHESDA CHURCH HARVEST FESTIVAL, RUNCORN. The celebration of the harvest home was held in the Bethesda Congregational Church, Runcorn, on Sunday, when sermons were preached appropriate to the occa- sion by the Rev. J. Simpson, pas:or. The church was nicely decorated with choice flowers and fruit. Amongst the items in an elat orate programme of music rendered by the choir were the sales :—"Trust in God," given by Mr. W. Gowanlock,and 'The Holy City," by Mr. E. Worrall. The collections were in aid of the Church funds.

TEMPERANCE LECTURES IN THE RUNCORN BOARD SCHOOL.

During the week Mr. J. J. Jakemans a lectnrer asso- ciated with the Band of Hope Union, has been giving lectures on temperance to the elder scholars attending the Runcorn Day Schools. The subject of the lectures was the effect of alcohol on the human system, and the lecturer aided his arguments in favour of abstinence by the exhibition of diagrams, illustrations, and experi- ments, showing the different effects of strong drink. The scholars are invited to compile essays on the subject matter of the lecture, and such lectures will be for- ward, d to the lecturer, who guarantees certificates of merit for the best composition.

ANNUAL SHOW.

Fine weather favoured the seventh annual show in connection with the Warrington and District Mare and Foal Society which was held in Walton ' Park on Thursday, by kind permission of Lady Greenall. It would be difficult, perhaps, to find a more desirable spot in the district for such a show than Walton Park, which in itself is a great attrac- tion and well repays a visit. When tired of gazing at the flue show of horses, the visitor was allowed to inspect the fine piggeries and stables belonging to Sir Gilbert Greenall,or to wander about the well- wooded park. A visit to the piggeries where some of the finest pigs in the country are to be seen, con- vinces one—as was aptly remarked—that even a pig-sty can be kept clean. With regard to show itselt,it may be stated that this year there was a diminution of entries frona 223 last year to 193 this year. This may be accounted for by the fact that Lord Egerton, and also Mr. Chadwick, of Runcorn, refrained from entering in order to give the others a better chance of gaining prizes. The show ground was opened shortly before ten o'clock, and the judging of shires and light horses was commenced and continued until one o'clock. The animals were led out into a ring, the outside of which was crowded with spectators, and this appeared to be the most attractive feature of the work of the morning. At two o'clock the judging of harness and tradesmen's turnouts was begun. Owing to the uniform excellence ' of the exhibits this was no easy task, and the same re- mark applies to the shires and lieht horses. The best shire mare in the show was adjudged to be "May Queen," the property of Mr. James Mercer, Halebank, near Widees, and she won the special prize of 2£5, as well as the silver cup for the best filly. The challenge cup for the best shire exhibit in the first fifteen classes was won by Mr. Joseph Leather, of Ditton Bank, Dit- ton. near Wines, who showed a fine chesnut mare, and he was closely run for the prize by Mr. Charks Taylor, of Hough Green with his "Hough Green Fashion." The silver cup for the beat mare with foal at foot, open to tenant farmers within seven miles of Warrington was awarded to Mr. George Priestner, of Bed wort:i Heath, for his hunter " In the class for the best and neatest tradesmen's turnout, there was much competi- tion, and some good turnouts were slauwn. Mr. John Cooper, of Cernhill, Warrington, succeeded in gaining the first prize; the Warrington Co-operative Society, second ; Wigan Coal and Iron Company, third; and Henry Milling and Company, Limited, fourth. The quality of the horses shown during the day was said to be quite up to the average. The efficials of the society are to be congratulated on the success of the show. They are as follows : Presi- dent : Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart ; vice-president : Syl- vanus Reynolds, Esq. ; committee: Messrs. Richard Ainscough, T. Ball, Win. Bradford, H. NOTES ON GARES AND

ATHLETICS.

FooTBiLL.

Last year's Lancashire and Cheshire emimpions mar- gurated their season in fine style by bratim: the TyMosley team in :loch a decisive manner. The Runcorn men had during the foreening week been in active train- ing and the mro who turned out to play for the club en Saturday were trained to the minute. If the members of tho team only take care to keep themselves in good condition and follow the instructi ,ns of their admirable captain, W. Faulkner,the cup will want some taking out of the town.

J. Faulkner proved the first to score this season and his struggle over the coveted line was wildly received. Houghton has evidently lost none of his goal kicking powers as his attempt on Faulkner's effort was highly successful And was the signal for another outburst of applause. Later on in the game Houghton took over a penalty kick which also ended satisfactorily. The ster- ling Runcorn back by the end of the afternoon's play quite demonstrated that he had got well into harness and was going strong.

OUR LOCAL CHURCIIES-WARRINGTOg.

WYCLIFFE F.S.A. SOCIETY.

The meeting en Sunday being the first in another half year, there was a very good attendance. Mr.G.Walling- ton was chairman and delivered a brief address on trust log in God under all circumstances. The speaker was Mr. J. A. Yonge, M.A., of Wakefield, the eldest son of the Rev. J. Yonge, who delivered a much appreciated address, his subject being “Christianity, the only true socialism."ln support of his proposition Mr.Yonge asked his hearers to listen to the voice of the Bible and the voice of history. In order to better understand the power of Christianity with regard to society, they must con- sider the power and method of Christianity as it affected the individual. It was indeed, one of the strongest evi- dences of the truth of Christianity that not one of Christ's contemporaries, not one even of His bitterest enemies, ever found a flaw in the character of the Son of Man. This •as the ideal which God in His word assured us was possible, and which was His will that we should attain. The attaining of this ideal was not and could a be the work of a day. The process was one of slow and steady growth. God had been pleased to give us not only a picture of the ideal man, but also a picture of the ideal society. After tbe outpouring of the Holy Spirit on the day of Pentecost, they were told that the early dis- ciples had all things in common, and sold their posses- sions and goods, and parted them to all men, as every man had nod. Here was a pic- ture of the ideal society. This was the ideal at which society must aim. But, as in the case of the individual so in the case of the society,the attainment of the ideal was not and could not be the work of a day. Society must reach its perfect state by a process which was slow but sure. The speaker in endeavouring to prove that this ideal society was the outcome of the Christian religion, quoted from the writings of Mr. Fr.iude, and Mr. Lecky, showing what was the state of society during the period immediately preceding the birth of Christ,also that which existed during the period of persecution of the early Christians. Self seemed to be annihilated. The boundaries of class went down before these new relationships, which overmastered the strong- est iestincts of men's minds. The new religion was a proclamation of the universal brotherhood of man. The affection which the members have to one another, the devotion of all to the general welfare, the spirit of infinite tolerance for any weekness or in quality, the presence everywhere of the feeling of actual brother- hood—these were the outward features of all the early Christian societieties. But like the individual, society had to come down frem the heights, and to enter upon a slow and steady march before it could again attaie the same level. In answer to the queetion, is progress being made? the speaker said they were bound to admit that since the days of Christ there had been a gradual but continuous growth of humanitarian feeling among the Western races, and this had reached its high- est development in the times in which we were living. This develepment which was going on, this progress to- wards the ideal society was not due, as some would say, to an increase of intellectual power but was essentially religious in its character. It was not so much a matter of the mind as of the heart. Society wee to-day nearer to its ideal state than it had been since the days of those early Chrstians of whom hey bad spoken. Our social tiroeress fonnded on Christianity, was baced on the teaching of Jesus Christ, received its strength and power to grow from His gospel. This gospel was capable of being embraced by each of them and therefore each could work in such a way as to help on the attainment of that ideal society which they all desire. They must display the spirit of Christ which was the spirit of love. Miss Fieldhonee added to the seccess of the service by singing a solo. Messrs. Brietow and Owen also took trt in the service. The President announced that over 70 new members had joined the seciety that day.

3 FROM THE STAGE TO THE CROSS."

LECTURE AT WARRINGTON.

Mr. Henry Thorne, evangelist, of Exeter Hall, London, visited Warringten on Tuesday, and additional interest was lent to is visit by the fact ttrat in his youth Mr. [borne played on the stage in this town, his father, many years ago, being the lessee of the Warrington Theatre. A bible reading was given by Mr. Thorne at three o'clock in Wycliffe Congregational Church, and at night he told the story of his life to a large congregation under the title of "From the stege to the Cross." Mr. R. Garnett, J.P., occupied the chair. After prayer by the Rev. John Yonge, the Chairman briefly introduced the lecturer. Be referred to the pleasure with which he listened to Mr. Thorne's bible reading, and said he was I glad to see that large representative assembly present that night.—Mr. Thorne said he had decided to give them the history of his career rieht from the very begin- ' ning. It was in the great city of Manchester that he first saw the light, bat on account of his father becoming connected with theatrical speculations he was removed to London when only ten months old. In a most inter- e.sting manner, with a rich vein of humour, the lecturer proceeded to relate his first impressions of life, and afterwards told of his first appearance on the stage at Margate in " The Illustrious Stranger." The lecturer's memories of great actors and his experiences in various centres of industry were attentively followed. In War- rington, he said, some people would remember his father, who engaged Mr. Barry Sullivan to appear in a J series of Shakesperian representations. On the occasion of one of the evenines of his engagement, Mr. Sullivan appeared In " Macbeth," and those who had read that stirring drama would know that there were eight ap- paritions that had to pass across the stage in the ghost scene. The only person he (Mr. Thorne) could find to undertake the dutiee of the ghost on the occasion to which he refer' ed was a nervous young man. He dressed him in a night shirt, and covered his face with I chalk, and instead of putting in his hand a little glass he put a big looking glass with a couple of draws in it. He j could assure them he looked much like a ghost. (Laugh. I ter') He remembered how the gravity of Mr. Barry Sullivan save way when the ghost fell through a trap ' door on the stage and landed in a beer barrel underneath (Laughter.) Memories had crowded upon him as he had walked through the streets of Warrington that day. He had kept going back to it in imaginetion,and fancied he was living over again. Through the instrumentality of two young men who were desirous of going on the stage he was led on a Sunday evening to attend the ser- vices in the Presbyterian dhurch beyond the Central Station. He could not remember the sermons but he heard one remark there which was destined to make a deep impression upon him. He fancied he saw that preacher now saying " there are some people wile love the newspaper better than the t3ilele." When he first heard that remark hc said to himself " that is . me." After al!uding to his conversion, he said he felt at the thee that he must sever his connection with the stage. He was there to testify to the resolution he had made thirty-six year , ago, by Divine help, and he had stood faithfully to it to that hour. He had then, while a youth, made up his mind not to go back to the stage, but also not to go to his home. One thing meant the ether. Efe had been trained for the stage. He knew no trade or handicraft by which he could earn a living. He took a position as a clerk, but he did not rhink he was particularly quali- fied for that position, as they would say if they saw his handwriting. (Laughter.) He gave up being a clerk and took to preaching. He wee invited to go to New- castle-on-Tyne. From Newcastle he went to Leeds as Secretary of the Y.M.C.A.,a position he afterwards gave up in order to be an evangelist. With respect to the stage, he said he thought the theatre was no place for him. It was borne in upon him that the only right side of a theatre was the outside. But he did not be- lieve that actors and actresses were the most degraded people in the world. (13ear,hear.) He was not there to utter a libel against people connected with the stage, many of whom he respected and loved. But he looked on the moral influence and he did not think it was good. If it wee unfit for his daughter to take part in some of the theatres,then it was not good for him. (Hear.hear.) —The Chairman was thanked for his services on the motion of the Rev. E. T. Reed,seconded by the Rev. John Yonge,and the trustees of Wycliffe Church were also thanked for the gratuitous use of the building, on the I proposition of Mr. Thorne.

WALKING MATCH BY A WA.REINGTON NAVVY. A record was 3reated on Saturday by a Warring- ton navvy, named James Robinson, who-undertook the task of walking from Sheffield to Warrington with a w'neellanrrow. The time set to cover the distance was twelve hours, and he started from Sheffield at half-past six in the morning. Robinson gamely plodded on through the inclement weather which beset him on his journey, and arrived in Warringtop at twenty minutes past six,followed by a crowd of admirers. He was in front of the stated time by ten minutes. SiE GILBERT GREENALL WITH THE BELVOIR HOUNDS.

A contemporary says ;—" Sir Gilbert Greens% the new Master of the Belvoir Hounds, and Ben Cappell, kis new hNtsman, accompanied by Mr. C. Oreenall, drew tEir first coverts in the Belvoir domains recently, and a jolly morning was spent in the woodlands surrounding the Duke of Rutland's famous castle. There were plenty of foxes, scent was good, and the ;Tort, from a cubbing point of view, was about perfect. One cub saved his brush by going to ground after being chased about for three-quarters of an hour ; but a second one was not so fortunate, for although he kept up for 15 minutes in front of 30 couples of dog hounds he was eventually rolled over. The following morning hounds were taken to Hallwell Mouth,and found a grand show of foxes in the curiously shaped covert. There was again a good scent, and the hounds ran nicely, hunting three cubs to gyound,and finishing up with a Adrn across the Behoir Vale to Wartnaby Stone Pits, where they were stopped. EA.RLESTOWN.

As stated a fortnight ago, Mr. Harwood, who for 28 years acted as chief foreman at the Wagon Works of the London and North-Western Railway Company, has retired from the service of the Company,on a well-earned pension. It was decided to snark his retirement by showing in a substantial and practical manner, the esteem and appreciation in which he was held by tX,e workmen. Aecordingly, collectors were chosen and v, committee appointed, and a very substantial sum was , got together, sullicient to enable the committee to pur- chase a gold chronometer centre seconds stop watch,and geld guard and pendant for Mr. Harwood, and an electro plated tea and coffee service with tray for Mrs. Harwood. Mr. Harwood finished his duties at the works on Friday evening, and on Saturday evening,the articles named were presented to him in the Viaduct Institute. _ _ _

The proceedings commenced with a knife and fork tea at the Viaduct Hotel, to which 111 were present. After tea an adjournment was made to the Institute, which was very soon filled. Mr. Joseph Emmett presided, and he was accompanied on the platform by Mr. Harwood, Mersa& Gilmour, Tarbuck, Bolton, Clare, Davies,Meiris, Mottram, Warburton, Pearson (committee) and Mr. Porter (secretary). What proved an excellent entertain- trent was then proceeded with, all the artistes having readily given their services, and it is only fair to say, that they did their utmost to make the concert a suc- cess. The programme opened with a piano solo by Mr. T. Gilmour, who very kindly also accompanied through- out the concert. Mr. S. Swann followed in fine style with the song The Alarm,' and Miss Conway, who was in splendid voice, gave a capital rendering of the son " All Hallow e'en." The song, " Mona," was given by Mr. J. Nichols, in his usual finished style and he was warmly applauded. Miss Matter received due recogni_ tion for her able rendering of the song" Flight of ages,' which she sang remarkably well. Mr. J. Isherwood proved himself a clever performer on the fairy bells, and his selection was redemanded. The appropriate song, "In Friendship's name," was very nicely sung by Mr. J. Gibbons, and he was loudly recalled. The humorous song " Born unlucky," by Mr. J. Huddleson, was, of course, encored and he sang an extra verse. The Chairman then proceeded with the real business of the even;.ng, viz, the presentation to Mr. Harwood. He said he was sorry he hadpeen placed in that position, as he bad no doubt many oT them had been expecting some great man to make the presentation, but as he was one of the men who had been acquainted with Mr. Har- wood ever since he came to Earlestown, the committee thought he was the most likely person to perform the ceremony. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Harwood came in the year 1867, early in August, as the outdoor foreman. He got through the work at that time so well, that in Octo- ber of the same year he was appointed foreman over the new work. Perhaps some of them would remember at that time that it was a little different to what it is now. It was work then, which some of them would know something about. He himself remembered on a cold frosty morning, the sweat was running down into his boots. (Loud laughter.) In the following year, Mr. Harwood bad don* his work so well that he was made chief foreman. (Applause.) That was in the year 1868, and he thought they would all agree with him that he had not made a bad one. (Loud applause.) The presents before him spoke for themselves, and showed that Mr. Harwood must have heel the kind feeling of the work- men. (Hear, hear.) He thought there was some credit due to Mr. 'Arwood for his efforts in connection with that room, and the grounds outside. Mr. Harwood had been connected with the Institute long before there was an Institute. (Laughter.) He made a slight mistake there, but he did not mean the present Institute. They had an Isstitute across the road for many years. Some of them would remember going to the penny readings , and concerts which used to be held in the long room. He thought there was some praise due to Mr. Harwood. in connection with the cricket club? Where would it have been if it had net been for him. (Hear, hear.) Look at the cricket field, how nicely it was laid out. And then there was the statuary They had had many a row to get these things, and there had been thunder and lightening, but when the sun shone again they came through, and he had no doubt they would all agree with him, that Mr. Harwood had been a jolly good fellow. (Cheers.) When Mr. Harwood looked at that beautiful watch, he would remember the many happy hours he had spent with his colleagues, and he (the chairman) hoped be would live long to enjoy it in health and happiness. He then formally presented Mr. Harwood with the watch and chain. The watch bore the following inscription : "Presented to F. Harwood, Esq., by the officials and workmen employed at the London and North-Western Railway Company's Waggon Works, Earlestown, on his retirement, after 28 years' service as Chief Foreman, September, 1896." Turning to Mrs. Harwood, the Chairman said he felt proud to have the honour of pre- senting those beautiful articles to her. He had known her for over 29 ...years, and he knew she was a good- tempered, sweet, smiling lady. (Hear, bear.) He had never seen her in a bad temper, and he knew she had been a great help to her husband. (Hear, hear.) Some of the committee of tbe Institute who had been along with him when they had a little extra work in connec- tion with the soirees, knew that she had come forward and helped them willingly, and worked from 'morning until night. He hoped all the young men present would be fortunate enough to get as good a wife as Mr. Har- wood had, and he hoped she would have health and prosperity to enjoy the beautiful things. He had been wondering what Mr. Harwood would do, when he finished at the works, and he thought the best thing he could do would be to put up for a member of Parlia- ment. (Laughter.) He then formally presented the service to Mrs. Harwood. On the tea pot was the fol- lowing inscription :—" ?resented to Mrs. Harwood by the officials and workmen employed at the London and North-Western Railway Company's Waggon Works, Earlestown, on the retirement of her husband, as chief foreman, as a testimonial of appreciation and esteem, September 1896." The musical portion was then proceeded with. Mr. J. Isherwood delighting the audience with his really clever performance on the musical glasses. This alone was a treat, and the audience showed their appreciation by a vociferous encore, to which Mr. Isherwood responded. Mr. J. Gibbons followed with "Close to the Threshold," and Miss Mather gave a pretty interpretation of the song "Star of Bethlehem." Mr. J. Nichols was quite at home in the song "My sweetheart when a boy," and it was well received by the audience. Mr. Harwood, replying on behalf of himself and wife, said he thought his first duty would be to propose a vote of thanks to the Chairman for the excellent man- ner in which he had occupied the chair that night, and for the kind way in which he had referred to Mrs. Har- wood and himself. (Hear, hear.) He also ought to mention that the Committee had provided an excellent , entertainment and that they deserved the best thanks of

that gathering. (Applause.) And to his fellow work- men he expressed his sincere and heartfelt thanks for the exceedingly handsome testimonial they had been pleased to present to his wife and himself. They accepted them in the same kind feeling in which he was sure they had been given, and he wished to assure those present that they would be prized above any articles he and Mrs. Harwood possessed, as they knew that they were the unanimous gift of his friends and fellow workers, whom he had had the honour of working with for the past 29 years, and from whom during that time he had received the greatest kindness and courtesy. (Applause.) It had been no easy task on his part to break away from them, but now that he had ceased to be their chief foreman he trusted that the kind feeling which had always existed might be continued in their private life. (Loud ap- plause.) He had always felt it a pleasure and a pride to be in the employ of the London and North-Western I Railway Company, the largest, and said Eo be, the best managed line in the kingdom. He could assure them it was no flattery on his part when he stated that he left behind him as good a staff of men—as steady and indus- trious—as could be found ia any railway works in the United Kingdom. (Cheers.) In conclusion he would again thank them for their great kindness towards his wife and himself that nielat. (Loud applause.) Miss Conway then sang in excellent taste the song "At the Ferry," and was • loudly applauded. Mr. S. Swann was recalled for his flue interpretation of the song "The Sentry," and Mr. J. Huddleston had also to make the usual second appearance after singing a humorous song. Mr. W. Gilmour then rose to propose a vote of thanks to the artistes. They were, he said, all local, and all voluntary, and he wished to recommend to Mr. Emmett that when the Institute Committee wanted good singers they could get them in Earlestown. He might say that he had not enjoyed any concert more than he had the one that evening. (Applause.) Mr. W. Davies seconded the proposition. He f elt double pleasure in being one of the committee appointed to organise the concert. He could, he said, quite bear out what had been said with regard to Mr. Harwood's doings in connection with the Institute, at least for a number of years. If he could advance the men's inter. eats in any way,he was always ready to do it. Mr.Har- wood had said he and his wife would appreciate the presents more than anything else they possessed, bet he thought there was something more, and that was tbe satisfaction of knowing that he had done his duty to em- ployees and employed. (Loud applause.) The mltion was put and carried with acclamation. Mr. W. ,Clarc proposed a vote of thanks to the Chair- man, and eulozised Mr. Ha. wood in a few well chosen remarks. _ -

Mr. James Bolton seconded, and after the singing of " Auld Lang Syne." and "The National Anthem" the audience dispersed. • At the final meeting of the Committee on Tuesday evening, it was found that there was a surplus ot 10s. 6d., and it was decided to expend this in purchasing a pipe and case for Mr. Harwood. Votes of thanks were also passed to Mr. Joseph Emmett (chairman) Mr. Porter (secretary), and Messrs. Tarbuck and Gilmour (collec- tors), for their service in connection with the presenta- tions.

THE LATE ALDERMAN SMITH J.P.

The Chairman read a letter he had received from Mrs. ' Smith, of Brookside, as follows: " August 25th. Please convey to the Urban District Council the heartfelt thanks of my family and myself for the resolution of sympathy and condolence in our deep sorrow and affliction. Ws e have been deeply touched with this handsome recogni- tion of my dear husband's services as a member of the of the Councii,which we appreciate most highly because we know his zeal for the welfare of the township and his interest in the business of the Council vas so keen that during the time that he thought he was convales- cent nothing gave him greater pleasure than to have the newspaper reports of the proceedings of the Council read to him.

SCHOOL ATTENDANCE COMMITTEE.

The minutes of this committee,the chief item of which was an account of ET 183 4d. on salaries account, were passed on the motion of Mr. Wood, seconded by Mr. Simpson. DEPUTY SANITARY INSPECTOR'S REPORT.

The Deputy Sanitary Inspestor (Mr. John Gre")ry) in his monthly report stated that the monthly tolls for street hawking were £4 135. 2d. ; market and stallage, £44 4s. id. ; total, £lB 17s. 3d.—including £6 ss..tor Newton Fair, against £7 35. last year And £l6 above the usual inarket tolls for the race week,which with the £26 3s. ed. for the May Queen event, makes £42 33. 6d., against £25 last year. Newton Fair,the inspector adds, seems to be dying out, and I think, if such a thing is possible, it would be better if arrangements could be made t• hold the fair in some field near to High-street, and have only one day in August as in May. It could be worked cheaper and would cause less friction with the public The report was adopted by the committee, ex- cept that part relating to the holding of Newton Fair be- ing held in some other place than High-street.

• COMPLAINT BY MR. GILMOUR

A complaint was made to the Highways Punlic Works and Sanitary Committee by Mr. Gilmour about some person keeping pigs in such a manner as to cause nui- banee. The Medical Officer of Health and the Inspector of Nuisances were instructed to visit the premises complained of (situate in Crew-lane near Newton Com- mon),and to report to tile committee at their next inlet- Lug.

THE DEPUTY NUISANCE INSPECTOR.-

The Highways,Public Works, and Sanitary Committee resolved that in future the Nuisance Inspector submit the monthly report of the Sanitary Department instead of his deputy as hitherto; also that the application of Mr. Gregory, deputy nuisance inspector,for leave of ab- sence froa business for ten days for a holiday from the 7th of September, was acceded to.—The Council con- firmed these recommendations. PROPOSED NEW BUILDINGS. Building plans were considered by the Highways, Public Works, &c., Committee, with reference to which it was resolved that the plan of Messrs. T. and R. Cross of Longford-street, Warrington, for 24 cottages on the north-west side of Athol-street be not passed ; and that the plan of Mr. W. Hales.of Brunswick-road, tor a bake- nouse and stable off Portland-street, be approved and passed.—The recommendations were confirmed.

MISCELLANEOUS. The Highways, Public Works and Sanitary Commit- tees' minutes of August 17th also contained the follow- ing :—Letter, dated July 24th, 1896, read from the Law Clerk anent the power of the Council to issue licenses for street hawking in lieu of taking tolls-. The Clerk was ordered to obtain further information from other local authorities having similar powers, and lay before the committee ; resolved: That a sufficient number of penny-in-the-slot locks be purchased for the sanitary conveniences en the market; that iron posts and ropes already provided for the protection of foot passengers and property during the holding if the Niro in High- street, Newton, be extended from the Blue Bell Inn to junction of road leading to Newton Mere and High. street ; that the tender of Messrs. Waring Brothers for the supply of Brinscall best 6inch paving setts at 13s.per ton, as may be required by the Council for one year,from the Ist of August, 1896, to July 31st, 1897, be recom- mended to the Council for acceptance. The Clerk was directed to write to Messrs. Richard Evans and Co., of Haydock Collieries, drawing their attention to the unsat- isfactory state of the footpath at the bottom of Fair- cleugh-street and the matter of the toll collector having taken possession of certain articles from the couveyance belonging to Mr. E. Lyon, of St. Helens, mineral water manufacturer, whose servant had refused to pay the amount of toll demanded,was reported to the committee. —The minutes of the committee were passed without comment on the motion of the Chairman, seconded by Mr. McMahon.

MAKING OF STREETS.

At a meeting of the Highways, Public Works and Sanitary Committee held on Monday afternoon (Septem- ber 7th) it was resolved that the tender of Mr. Hutton, of Bowden, for the making of Mersey-street, Walmsley- street, Birley-street and Stanley-street be accepted, sub- ject to the reterence being given by him to the satisfac- tion of the Surveyor and the Chairman of tba Committee. —The resolution was approved of.

GAS AND WATER COMMITTEE.

A meeting of this committee was held on August 17th, Mr. Borron in the chair. The Chairman incidentally mentioned the water question, and stated that a special meeting of the committee would be called, when the whole matter would be laid before them for their con- sideration. The questioa of the chairmanship of this committee was also mentioned, but it was agreed to let the matter stand over until the next meeting. —The Gas Manager (Mr. Joseph Swann) in his report stated he had had a deal of trouble lately from the street lamps being wilfully broken, principally about the Newton Common, but in other parts of the district also. This caused a great waste ef labour and material, be- sides destroying the usefulness of the lamps. Something should be done to prevent it if possible. The illumin- ating power of the gas has been kept up to the usual standard, and all other matters at the works were go- ing on satisfactorily. Consumption of water for past month had been as follows :—Newton Bridge meter, 3,790.000 gallons; viaduct meter, 2,200.000 gallons ; Parkside meter, 11,000 gallons ; total, 6,001,000. Cor- responding month last year, 5,843,000; increase,lsB,ooo. —The cemmittee recommended that the Clerk issue notice warning persons against damaging the gas lamps, and that they be posted in the district ; that the tender of Messrs. Hardman and Holden, Limited, of Miles Ilatting,Mancliester,of 325. 3.1. per ton for surplus tar produced at the gas works for the period of one year from September Ist, 1896, to August 31st, 1897, be ac- cepted, subject to their carting the same at their own expense from the gas works ; also that the matter of obtaining a loan in respect ot expenditure already in- curred, and of further proposed outlay on capital account for extension of gas. di tributing plant, be re- ferred to the Finance Committee. At a further meeting of the committee on Monday, the 7th September, it was resolved that the gas engineer be authorised to procure the necessary incandescent lamps for the lighting of Market.-street, Bridge-street, and Queen-street.—The committee's minutes were confirmed on the motion of the Chairman.

===== 0002601_18960912_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
FINANCE.

The Finance Committee's minutes stated that it ap- peared by the Treasurer's books that the following balances were in his hands in respect of the under mentioned accounts, viz: :—General department £4,501 53 2d.. gas £245 163 e id., water £216 is. 2d., school attendAnce committee account £55 63.5 d., total balance in treasurer's hands £5,018 83. 10d. The committee resolved that the Council be recommended to draw cheques upon the several Treasurer's accounts, amount- ing to the sum of £1,584 103., and that the matter of obtaining a loan for the Gas Department for extensions of gas distributing plant be referred to Mr.Ormand,Mr. li•rron, and Mr. Collingweod.—Approved of on the pro- position of Mr. Ormand, seconded by Mr. McMahon.

The General Purposes Comm—it-te-e-Wco-mmended,and it was agreed that the tender of Mr. J. C. Atkinson, of Bridge-st., Earletown, for the winding and keeping in thorough repair of the Town Rail clock for one year from September lb;t, 1896, to August 31st, 1897, for the sum of £3 101. be accented.

WELLINGTON PICNIC CLUB, EARLESTOWN,

On Wednesday night the members of the Welling- ton picnic club, wound up their season: with a hot pat ditiner at the Wellington Hotel. There were 47 members present and Coe dinner being excel- lently served, was thoroughly enjoyed. The after proceedings were of a convivial character, Mr. J. C ad wick presiding and songs, &c, being given by Messrs. Chew, ()Neil, Gibbons. Shaw, Mellor, Dickenson,, Cullen, Dolan and Chadwick. Selec- tions were given by the club band.

LAST WEEK'S TRAFFIC.

During the seven days ended Friday,September 4, 138 sea-going car 2,, vessels,with an aggregate net registered tonnage ot 44,337 tons, entered and cleared from the port ot Manchester. Amongst the more important de- part we were stelmers of the Ocean line tn China and Japan, of the Gulf line to Australia, and of the Lamport and Solt line to Brazil. Messrs. Langland's steamer Princess Irene, from Leith, Dundee, and Aberdeen, landed a thousand Scotch sheep at Trafford Wharf, and nearly 200 head of cattle arrived from Dublin by the : steamer Bear.

SUICIDE OF AN IRONWORKER AT

WARRINGTON.

An inquest was conducted by Mr. S. Brighouse, on Saturday atternoon, in the Parade Room, Irlam-street, Warrington, on thl body of Hamblet Guest, an iron- worker, who resided at 22, West-street, and who hanged himself in an out-house in his yard on Thursday morn- ing last. The first witness called was Emma Guest, who stated that the deceased was her husband. He was 50 years of age. Ile was stopped from his work at the forge the previous week. He did take drink occasionally but he did not leave his work in consequence. She believed it was only a temporary stoppage at the works. He was upset about it. On Wednesday night he went to bed. He talked about being discharged. He got up at two o'clock in the morning complaining of pains in the head. He had often done that before and she also got up with him as a rule. It did not alarm her when he went out. He had never threatened to take his life and she never suspected it. The discharge from the works had something to do with it.—By In- spector Spinks : The deceased had not lost time through drink. He was no a man that seemed likely to take his life. BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES

THE ATTEEPTED SUICIDE AT WARRINGTON. At the Warrington Borough Court on Monday, before Mr. F. Monks (chairman) and Mr. J. W. Hayward, a middle-aged man named Peter Quinn, 17,Town Hill,was brought up on remand charged with attempting to com- mit suicide by cutting his throat with a penknife.—The Chief Constable stated that the defendant went heme drunk on Saturday week and made a rambling statement that he had been "backing" horses and lost. He then .went upstairs and was afterwards heard to say, "I have done it now." But he had not done it effectually, hav- ing only made a slight wound on his throat with his penknife. He (the Chief Constable) was informed by the warder from the gaol that the defendant had been per- fectly rational and reasonable in his conduct there. He suggested that the defendant should be discharged, and he trusted that it would be a lesson to him in the future.—The defendant was accordingly discharged. A CHARLIE Or FALSE PRETENCES.—A MISUNDER- STANDING.

At the Warrington Police Court on Friday, before Al- derman Wright, Dr. Gornall, and Alderman Sutton, Joseph Johnson, 23, Winwick-st. horse dealer, was charged with obtaining by false pretences from Thomas Fryer, £2O with intent to defrand.—Mr. C. H.Unsworth appeared for the prosecutor,and stated that as there was a misunderstanding in the case be would not call any evidence.—Mr.H. Woods represented defendant,and said the case was simply a transaction which took place through the sale of a horse under a warranty. The pro- secutor felt himself aggrieved. There was an offer made some time ago that if he was not satisfied with the horse which corresponded with the warranty, they would be prepared to take the horse back. It was really a case which should never have been brought in that court,but should have been a civil action.—The charge was with- drawn.

A SIISPICIOLTS CASE.

At the Borough Court on Thursday, before Mr. Chand- ley (chairman), and Alderman Sutton, a forge labourer, named Bernard O'Rourke, of 17, Dolmans-lane, was charged on remand with frequenting the Crown and Sceptre yard with intent to commit a feleny.—Prisoner said he was drunk and knew nothing about it.— the Chief Constable (Mr. Luke Taltot) said the prisoner had beennemanded &Ince Monday. About 25 minutes after midnight on Sunday morning, P.C.'s Millington and Rylett were on duty in Buttermarket-street,in the neigh- bourhood of Percival's Fold, when they saw a man com- ing down the Crown and Sceptre yard in his stocking feet. On seeing the police, he ran back and was pursued by them. They found him concealed behind a handcart and he could give no acc,aunt of himself. His pockets were bulky and in them were found a con- siderable number of eggs and full-packets of cigarettes. Behind the handcart were also a number of bottles con- taining aerated water. The articles were no doubt taken from some small shop in the neighbourhood but as the prisoner lived there and belonged to that peculiar, got to say undesirable class, there was no doubt that the gheelo.eppr was afraid to inform the police. He (the Chief Constable) submitted there was sufficient evidence to commit the prisoner to gaol with having been there with intent to commit a felony.—This statement was corroborated by P.C. Millington, and P.C. Rylett, and the prisoner was sent to prison for one month, hard labour.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 12, 1896.

UNANIMOUS _DECISION.

As there exists differences of opinion among many practitioners as to the real cause and origin of riseuma- tiam, and to obtain a decision resulting from the I practical experience of eminent celebrities, a Medical Author wrote to several hundred prominent and noted phyicians, asking there to give their opinion upon the important question. They unanimously replied that all forms of rheumatism and gout were a result of an ex- cess of uric acid in the blood, which they attributed to imperfect action of the digestive organs and the kidneys, concluding by saying their experience proved that rheu- matism could only be cured by systematic treatment ; resto ing the kidneys to their normal state, and the di- gestive organs to their normal action, thus gradually expelling the excess of uric acid from the blood. The longer these disturbahces and abnormalities exist, the more difficult it becomes to relieve the symptoms of rheumatism. _ .

As a special remedy, Warner-5 Safe Cure is recom- mended, as in all such eases it produces the desired effect, restoring the digestive organs and kidneys to a healthy action—oxidizimr, the uric acid to urea—which is a prominent and normal constituent of the excretions —thus successfully curing rheumatism and gout in their various forms. This proves that the popular opinion which prevails among many, that these diseases can be cured by ex- ternal application, linanrents,. ointments, ombrocation, plasters, etc., is erroneous. This, undoubtedly, accounts for the wonderful success which always follows the use of Warner's Safe Cure, even in the most obstinate cases of rheumatism, as is plainly shown by the experience of Major McMurray, Springfield House, Killuney, Armagh, who says :—" I consider it my duty to make public the following statement. I was a martyr to rheumatic gout from 1865 until April, 1888 ; being laid up with it regu- larly every six months, sometimes for weeks and some- times for months. In April, 1888, I commenced to take Warner's Safe Cure. I found relief after the fourth bottle. I took twenty-one bottles. Since I stopped tak- ing the medicine, in July, 1888, I have not had the slightest indication of rheuMatic gout." " August 29th, 1894.—1 have pleasure in confirming above statement, made four years ago, and adding, my old enemy has not visited me since."

RUNCORN PETTY SESSIONS.

Moivbalr.—Before Mr. J. Norman and Mr. I. W. Lightburn. OBSCENNI LANOITAGE.—Ann Corcoran was sent to Knuts- ford for 14 days' hard labour for using obscene language in Church-st.. Runcorn, on August 29th. CRUELTY TO CANAL BOAT HORSES.—William Forster was summoned for working two ponies on the 29th August whilst in an unfit oonditien, and John Forster was charged with causing the same to be worked. Both defendants were batmen.— PC. Kendrick said he was on duty in Halton- road on the date specified aboat noon, and saw the two de- fendants driving the two ponies attached to a boat. On examining them he found that one, a grey pony, had wounds on ea .1h of the shoulders while the other a brown one, had a wound on the off shoulder. Blood and matter were coming from all the wounds,and the collars were stained with the same substances.—lnspector Bilson, of the Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Animals, stated that he examined the ponies on the 31st August and found them suffering as described by the officer. The brown pony had two wounds on the off shoulder measuring 2 inches by 11, while the grey pony had a wound on one shoulder 2 by 3 inches and another en the other shoulder four by three inches. Both the wounds were deeply seated and of long standing, charged with matter and highly inflamed. The ponies were ago.l but in fair condition. He was shown the collars the animals had worn and they were both belly stained. The ponies had been stopped by a brother officer at Kidsgrove who warned defend Ints not to work them back to Runcorn. He had no hesitation in say- ing that to work the two ponies 57 miles in that condition was a most gross act of cruelty. Defendants pleaded guilty. but stated that the ponies were not in the condition that had been stated. The magistrates came to the conclusion that the case was a very had one, and fined the first defendant 15s, and costs and the second, for causing them to be worked, 20s. and costs. DRUNK AND DRlVlNO.—Williani Hammond was fined 10s. and 95. costs for being drunk in charge of a horse and carriage in Bridge-st., Runcorn, on the Ist fieptember.

PADGA.TE.

A LIVELY PARISH COUNCIL MEETING.

A special meeting of the Poulton-with-Fearahead Parish Council was held in the schoolroom on Friday evening week, when the chair was occupied by Mr. Wm. Royston. There were also present—Messrs. William Houghton, J.P., Henry Thornton, George Mather,James Mason, Joseph Roberts, and T. H. Granger. There was a large attendance of ratepayers.

===== 0002601_18960912_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SMALL-PDX HOSPITAL.

Mr. Thornton had given notice that ho should call the Council's attention to the fact that the Warrington Cor- poration had given instructions to their Surveyor to prepare plans of the proposed smallpox hospital to be erected at Pear Tree Farm, Houghton Green, and tho bringing of smallpox patients through their township. After reading a report of the proceedings of the Town Council relating to the matter, he said there was a very strong feeling outside the Council on the subject, which was an important one,and one which affected the town- ship materially. It affected property owners as well as occupiers. His opinion was that whether a man lived in a hut or a mansion he had a voice in the matter, and a meeting should be called to take the ratepayera'opi nions. Mr. Royston (interrupting) stated it was a matter which had been discussed bv the old Cpuncil, and they had been watching it all along. The Chairman was making some further remarks when Mr. Thornton said : " If you will allow me, lam on my feet."—The Chairman : I must ask you to keep within bounds. It is not a ques- tion that you should keep us all the evening about. I wish you to curtail your remarks.—Mr. Thornton : You don't know how long I am going to speak. I have a right to speak on any proposition, and am within my bounds. I shall obey you as chairman if you call me to order in a respectful way. If you don't allow me to speak here I will call a meeting on the green and speak. Ilhave a right to speak on a motion. I never knew a chairman who knew his business to call anyone to order as you have done me to-night.--The Chairman again called Mr. Thornton to order, and asked him to keep within bounds, but Mr. Thornton continued : You, as chairman, are not in bounds, and if you don't keep within them we as a meetiog shall have to keep you more in bounds. Mr. Thornton moved "That the chairman of the Parish Council call a parish meeting in order to test the feeling and to receive tho instructions of the rate- payers in this matter."—Mr. Houghton said he would not sit there and hear those remarks made. They were out of order. The Chairman was supremo,and he should support him.—Mr.Thornton asserted that tho Chairman was not supreme.—Mr. Granger seconded the proposi- tion which was carried.

===== 0002601_18960912_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
GAS AND WATER.

On the proposition of Mr. Thornton, it was resolved "That the Clerk be instructed to communicate with the Warrington Corporation, asking them to grant the fav- our of laying gas and water mains to the extent of the new property in Cinnamon and Fearahead lanes, Fearn- head."

===== 0002601_18960912_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
A DRAINAGE QUESTION.

The following notice of motion stood in the name of Mr. Thornton :—" I also call the atttntien of the Coun- cil to my proposition of June 12th, relating to repairs and work done by the roadmen,hoth on and off the high road during last year, and to the action taken by Mr. William Houghton in reference thereto at the last High- way Board meeting, held on August 22 ad." Mr. Thorn- ton said much dissatisfaction exisced amongst the rate- payers, with reference to the matter referred to in the resolution aud they had a right to ask questions apper- tainine thereto. When lie was elected a member of that Couneil be was asked to bring the matter forward, and he had done so. Mr. Thornton then referred to the manner le which the subject had been previously raised at the Parish Council, and also to the resolu- tion which was passed at the Highway Board meeting on the motion of Mr. W. Houghton.— Mr. W.-Houghton said that when the resolution was submitted to the Council Mr. Thornton occupied the chair. He made use of his position as chairman and took advantage of that position to reflect upon their late wavwarden. He said this work had been done and the Parish Council had winked at it. He (the speaker) having been a member of tke old Council objected to the remark at the time. Their Chairman, he believed, had received the Highway Board Surveyor's report, and if it was satisfactory he thought an apology was due from Mr. Thornton to the Parish Council and the Highway Board for the statements he had made.—Mr. Royston,ia explaining, said the drains of the highway at time in question were all choked and the wreckage which han been washed down the incline of the railway bridge had got into this ditch snd the water could not flew. The Surveyor reported to the Board the state of things, Mr. Bennett,one of the parties concerned ,was very busy. and asked that the highway men might do the work and he would pay his share. The Finance Committee sanctioned this course and the work was done, and the parties charged accordinely. He hoped everyone would now be satisfied. Nothing had been smuggled, and there was nothine to be ashamed of.—An attempt was made to further continue the discussion, but the Chairman said : "It must close. I will not allow it to go any further. This Council has nothing to do with it at all." The meeting which was a lively one throughout, was lan unusually long one. GENERAL NEWS.

The application of the workmen in the engineering trade:. of the Manchester district for an advance of 23. per week in wages was considered on Tuesday at a representative meeting:of employers held in Manchester. It was unanimously resolved—" That, in the best inter- ests both of employers and employed, a general rise of wages in this district is net justified,would be inexpedi- ent, and cannot at the present time be conceded."

REVISING TaE LIST 3 AT WARRINGTON.

Mr. James Thomas Foard, revising barrister, dur- ing the passt week has revised the lists of Parlia- mentary voters,of burgesses, of county electors,and of parochial electors for the borough. The court held in the Council Chamber Town Hall,Mr.Clayton appeared on behalf of the Liberals and Mr.llolbrook represented the Conservatives. There were also present Mr. J. L. Tunstall (assstant overseer), Mr. E. C. Cooper (assistant overseer for Winwick), Yessrs. T. J. Garliett and F. R. Aukland (relieving officers). The Town Clerk was represented by Mr. Stanley Wilson, deputy-town clerk. CLAIM BY A BAILIFF. The Liberals made a claim on behalf of Robert Lewis, bailiff of the County Court, for a vote for 3V, Upper Rank-street. Questioned by Mr. Holbrook, Mr. Lewis said that Mrs. Harrison was the tenant af the heuse, but he resided there in a service capacity. He did not pay any rent, his wife performing certain ser- vices instead of paying it. fie was not compelled to live on tho premises and could leave them at any time. Mr Holbrook : What is this place used for ?—lt is simply a meeting place connected with the Charity Organisation Society.—Mr. Clayton remarked that Mr. Lew is performed certain services instead of paying rent. —Applicant said his wife did so. He was not compelled to live on the premises. He could leave them at any time he chose to do so.—The Barrister : There must be some agreement of tenancy. If it is an understood thing that these services are to be given it would be in the nature of a service qualification. It cannot be anything else.—The vote was not allowed. BANK QUAY COFFEE HOUSE MANAGER'S cwinl. Mr. John Burgess, manager of the British Workman coffee-house, Milner-street, at preseat only having a ser- vice qualification, applied for a m ,nicipal vote. In answer to Mr. Clayton, the applicant said there was no compulsory occupation. He had to commence business at a certain hour in the morning and he finished at a certain hour of the evening. He could go and live where he liked between those times.—The Barrister : It is still a service occupation.—Mr. Clayton : But there is no compulsory occupation.—The Barrister : I am not suggesting it, but he has a service to perform in connection with his occupation. He has to perform certain services. It is not necessary that he should be compelled to reside there. He gets his equivalent and pays no actual rental, and there is no coatract of ten- ancy.—Mr. Clayton : If he did not perform certain ser- vices be would have to pay the usual rent,—The Barris- ter : That is a matter of argument and suggestion that I cannot deal with. It is clear that the man's tenancy, as far as it is a tenancy at all, is conditional upon his performing the services he does perform.—Mr. Clayton : Within certain hours.—The Barrister : If he was dis- charged he would have to give up the occupation of the house. Well, that cannot make a tenancy except for services under the statute, which gives a service qaalifi- cation.—The application was not allowed.

A CONSERVATIVE OBJECTION. The Consetvatives objected to the vote of James Lawton,l2 Wellfield-street, on the ground that there was access between the dwelling-house of James Lawton and that of his father's which really constituted one house and not two.—Mr. Clayton: There are two dis- tinct tenants,and there is simply a means of access from one to the other for family convenience.—The Barrister: That does not make it more than one house. The only way out of the difficulty would be to obtain a service vote. He could not allow the vote as it stood, and the objection must be successful. A LODGER VOTE. Mr. Jeseph George Flasrison,so, Wilderspool-causeway, applied for a lodger vote, which was objected to by the Liberals.—ln answer to Mr. Clayton, Mr. Tunstall said the house was assessed at gross £l3 10s„ rateable £ll. 108. Applicant said he paid Bs. a week, and that sum included the bare furnished rooms. He had a bedroom to himself, and had his meals separately. There was another lodger in the house.—Mr. Clayton observed that Mrs. Hindley had already a vote for the house, and the gross rental was only £l3 10a.—Questioned by Mr.Foard, applicant said he had the joint use of a sitting-room with another tenant.—The Barrister : As the other ten- ant does not claim, I think this may be al- lowed, but It is rather a doubtful claim.- -Mr. Clayton: We cannot get £lO for the rooms where the gross is only £l3 10d.—The Barrister : I don't think you can very well. There is a difficulty about it if he only gets the joint use of the sitting room.—Further questioned, Mr. Harrison said that there were six rooms in the house. There were four persons in the house— the two lodgers mid Mrs. Hindley and her neice.—Mr. Holbrook: There is no Parliamentary vote for the house The Barrister: But lam pressed by the objection that there is not margin enough as the house is only £I3 10i. gross. The applicant has also only the joint use of a sitting room. The claim is rather doubtful, but I will allow it.

- The Court then adjourned until the following morn- ing.

THE TRADES CONGRESS.

The Trade Union Congress was opened at Edinburgh on Monday. The Lord Provost welcomed the delegates, and Mr. Cowey, chairman of the Parliamentary Commit- tee, replied. Mr. John Mallinson, secretary of the Edin- burgh and District Trades Council,was elected president. The Parliamentary Committee presented their report, in which it was stated that the past session had been a barren one as regarded measures for the benefit of the , working classes. Measures of a retrograde or question- able character had had attention, but it would be difficult to point to one really tangible and beneficial measure passed in the interests of labour. On the prin- ciple of the maxim that " Ghosts throw their shadows before them," it was very questionable whether the working-class community need entertain very high hopes of anything being done for them during the lifetime el , the present Parliament. The Committee desired to point to a new departure on the part of the Postmaster General and the Secretary for War, both Ministers hav- ing declined to receive deputations on behalf of Govern- ment employe 4 who complained of grievances. This was considered very unsatisfactory, and the moro so as the Secretary for War and other Ministers in the last Parlia- ment always showed the utmost courtesy and willingness to receive deputations. Mr. John Mallinson, president, delivered an inaugural address at the Trade Unions Congress at Edinburgh on Tuesiay. Mr. Mallinson gave the Socialists credit for the service they had rendered in calling attention to the conditi-ei of the workers, but said the Socialists must bear with those who could not see that the remedies they proposed were calculated to bring about the desired results, and who believed that the social millenium would come by &Trees, and only in proportion as more just idPas and kindlier fc2lings pervaded all classes. By all means Ir-r, them have a workers' political party, but let it he kept outside trade unionism. The fight of trade unionism in the future would be for a living wage and to establish the claim that, as labour was the chief factor in production, the remuneration of labour should be the first charge on production. The President's ad- dress was followed by a discussion on the report of the Parliamentary Committee,tho chief question raised being on the paragraph relating to the recent International Socialist. Workers, and Trade Unions Congress. It was decided that the Congress eould only identify itself with these international gatherings on the condition that the delegates represented bona-fide labour organisations and their numbers werr proportionate to the numbers they reprrsented. A resolution in favour of a legal eight-hour day was carried,an amendment to give an alternative of 48 hours per week being rejected. Resolutions were also passed dealing with employers' liability, and the law of conspiracy, and the poor law, and the Factory Acts.

FOOTBALL.

WARRINGTON v. SWINTON.

The Warrington list of fixtures for the present season commenced with a match with Swinton, which was played at Swinton on Saturday. Swinton are,of course, new converts to Northern tinionism,and it was unknown how their team would be composed under the new regime, as Valentine, Lewis, and a few other old players were very doubtful starters. The Committee had been importing very largely to prepare for emergencies, and some of their men were noted wanderers. Valentine's

position was much debated, and it was evident that the Committee had not expected him to play when they sel- ected the team, for his name did net appear in the list. On Saturday, however, he was advised by Salford to turn out for Swinton, and though he delayed his consent until the last half hour, he eventually decided to play, Lewis standing down for him. Contrary to expectations the Swinton team did not differ very much from last year, Atherton from Barton. Goodman from St. Helens and a good many other places, and Hanson from Tyldesley, Rochdale St. Clements, and Rochdale Hornets; and S. Hall from Brough- ton Rangers being the most noticeable changes. Barber was unable to play for Warrington, and O'Brien took his place,the teams being composed as follows :—Warring- ton : Heesorn,full back: O'Brien, Cross, Isher wood,and Lawless, three-qnarters ; Bate and Hig,ginbottom, half backs ; Taylor,Dakin, Barrett, Saunders, Fairhurst, An- drews,Thernton and Hill,forwards. Swinton : Makin, full back; Valentine, Goodman, Butler and Atherton, three•quarters ; Pearson and HAnson,half backs ; John- son,Enner, Greenhalgh, Evans,Tickle, Hall, Ainsworth, and Norton, forwards. Mr. J. H. Smith, of Widnes, re- feree. When Valentine entered the field he received a great ovation, but it was very evident that he did not feel very comfortable, after his quarrel with the com- mittee, Smack Bate did not win the toss.but the Swin- ton captain decided to face both wind and slope during the first half. Hill set the hall in motion in the presence of between 3,000 and 4,000 spectators. Hanson by a geed run and kick at once put Swinton on the aggressive, and for a few minutes, the" Lions" had the better of the argument. The pressure was re- lieved by a long kick in touch by O'Brien and for a long period from now, Swinton never got beyond half way. A grand chance was missed when Hiv,ginbottone failed to snake his mark right in front of the goal posts, but Warrington's hopes were raised when as the result of a good dribble, the ball was worked to the line, when Thornton unaccountably failed to draw first blood for Warrington. The attack was kept up, and Bate by making a feint at passing almost tricked the whole of his opponents, but was finally floored by Goodman. The attack was brought to an end by Higginbettom kicking too hard, and sending the ball dead. The ball had scarcely been kicked out, when Bate sent over the line, and was only just prevented by Makin from scoring, the latter touching down. Swinton at this stage were un- doubtedly having the worst of it, and Lawless at- tempted to drop a goal, but the ball refused to rise. Talentine new for the first time in the match became

prominent by a grand run, which almost resulted in a score, fleeeoen proving his downfall, but Swinton at length forced a minor. A fine dribble by Barrett, Thornton, and Fair- hurst followed the kick out,but unfortunately it came to nothing, and at half-time, Swinton led by a dropped goal to nil. Taylor made a 'plucky effort to break away, but was not well- supported, and Valentine by a long kick at onae trans- ferred play to the other end, where Goodman easily romped over. Pearson failed to kick a goal. The cheers for this performance had scarcely died away, when Atherton got in at the opposite corner. Butler had a try at the goal this time but failed likewise. This seemed to completely disorganise War- riegton and in another few minutes Valentine- got past all oppesition but Heesom, and when, the latter tried to bring him down, he had the impu dance to jump over the full back, and fell with the bal just over the goal line. Pearson for the second time failed to kick a goal. Warrington, stung with these re- peated reverses, now aroused themselves, and by a grand united effort, in which Taylor, Dakin, Saunders, and Andrews were very prominent, got the ball right to the Swinton line, where Valentine rushing up, kicked dead. The ball had, however, been in play, and a five wards scrimmage was ordered. Here Norton infringed the off-side role, and from the free kick allowed Heesome tipped the ball over the crossbar. Swinton were attacking as strongly as ever, and Lawless p.re- vented the doughty Valentine from scoring by running him in touch, and O'Brien stopped Atherton from get- ting further ahead. Swinton at length raised their score in a very luckly manner, for Pearson took a flying kick at the ball, and was as much astonished as everybody else when he saw the wind turn it rofind and send it between the posts. Goodman intercepted a pass from Fligginbottom after the restart,and looked a likely scorer, until Heesorn proved his downfall. Valentine again tried to drop a goal but the ball just went outside. This was Swinton's last effprt,and Warrington seemed to get their second 'A ind, after looking all over a beaten team. They commenced to play in a determined manner, and after a short run by Isherwood, Dakin got hold and was only floored on the line. From the ensuing scrimmage, pass- ing between Bate and Lawless enabled the latter to score a try, which Taylor failed to improve. Still attacking a brilliant round of passing between Bate, Cross, Isher- wood and Lawless was followed by the latter running from the quarter flag over the line. The Warringtonians claimed a try,but it was ruled that Lawless was in touch in goal, and thus one of the most brilliant flashes of play in the whole match went unrewarded.
