RUNCORN v. TYLDESLEY.

Runcorn met the Tyldesley organiz itien on Saturday and the opening match was exceptionally well attended. The visitors brought their full strength but Runcorn were short of Myers, Moores, Langley, Williams and Robinson, who were prominent players in last years' fifteen. When the teams lined up in the field they were composed as follows :—Runcorn : Houghton, back ; Butterworth, Richardson, Warder and W. Gayter, three- quarter backs ; J. Faulkner and Pendlebury, half backs; W. Faulkner. Walker, Hughes, Farmer, Lightfoot, Darl- ington, Taylor and F. Gayter, forwards. Tyldesley : Trove, back • Lawton, Evans, Miller and Price, three- quarters backs; J. Berry and W. Berry, half backs ; G. Woodward, Worthington Harris, R. Berry, Smith, W. Woodward, Tinker and Prescott, forwards. Mr. P. Speakman, J.P., C.A. president of the Runcorn club, kicked off, but before doing so accorded a hearty welcome to the visitors, and expressed an earn- est hope that the game would be charac- terized by the usual good feeling which had always existed between the two teams. Worthington received the ball from Mr. Speakman's kick,and returned to Warder. Immediately afterwards a free kick was awarded to the cup holders, which was taken in hand by Faulkner, with the result that play became located in the visitors' half. This, however, did not long con- tinue, for as the result of a series of rushes, the visiting forwards endangered the home lines. Runcorn defended in a stubborn manner, and thus kept their fortress in- tact. Houghton and Butterworth by judicious kicking gained relief, but quickly the Lancastrians were again on the offensive, and if it had not been for over eager- ness on their part they might have scored, for they had a couple of easy chances. Warder, by an effective punt, got the ball to touch near the centre, where hostilities were conducted for a few minutes. The visitors made another raid into the opposition camp bat were sent to the right about by a lofty kick by Richardson, to which Lawton replied. Warder securing possession of the oval saw Runcorn having the best of matters, and Houehton taking charge of a penalty kick almost succeeded in scoring. He experienced very hard lines, the ball just falling short of the uprights. The wearers of the green by this time had settled down to hard work, and were to he seen practising the art of passing, whereby they kept the visitors on the defensive. A beautiful passing movement by Butterworth, Warder, and Richardson re- sulted in last-named player racing over the line, passing at the right moment to J. Faulkner, who put the finish- ing touch to an excellent bit of combination. Tbis, the first score of the season, was the signal for loud and con- tinued cheerio., which was renewed when Houghton landed a goal, from a somewhat difficult position. The Lancastrians, who were by far the heaviest men, carried the scrimmages, with the result that they invaded the home lines, and on several occasions almost scored. Their final efforts, however, lacked finish. Houghton displayed the defensive powers for which he has lang had a reputation. Ultimately the ball was got away, and Walker, with a splendid dribble, took play to the opposite en.!, but his career was checked by Trow. Both teams played with the greatest determinati en, and matters became of an interesting and exciting character. Runcorn havieg another penalty kick accorded to them Houghton negotiated it almost with success, the oval dropping just under the bar. Thence to half time the game was carried on in more neutral territory. The score at the interval was, Runcorn, one gaol; Tyldesley nothing. Mr. Speakman kicked off en behalf of the visitors, to which Faulkner made but a feeble reply. The visitors, as the result of a grand display by their forwards, gave the home defence plenty to do. In the scrimmages they were very successful, but the back division were again at fault, they failing time after time to improve the opportunities given them by the brothers Berry. Through the splendid combination of Hughes, Warder, and Richardson relief came, and for several minutes hostilities were carried on in the visitors' quarters, Butterworth almost cross- ing the lines, he having passed Trow. Lawton was his stumbling block. After some further exchanges, Evans, Lawton, and the brothers Berry made tracks for the home citadel but Houghton was tc o much for them, he displaying his old form, and and with his huge punts finding touch, thus placing his colleagues in a good position. Tall kicking between the { respective custodians was the neat interesting feature, and in response to one by Trow,Houghton was compelled to touch down. The Cestrians put in good work, and { tried to increase their score. They kept on the aggres- { sive,and on several occasions experienced very hard { lines. Trow was so severely pressed that he had to ran back over his line. Sill the play continued in close proximity to Tyldesley's citadel, and although the visi- tors were awarded a penalty kick it proved of slight ad- vantage, for they were compelled to touch down twice in quick succession. Within three or four minutes of the close Houghton negotiated a penalty kick, and was suc- cessful in sending the ball between the uprights, a feat which was loudly applauded. The home team continued to hays the upper hand up to the time when the whistle blew, the score then standing Runcorn two goals (one penalty) Tyldesley nil.

DEATH OF MR. RICHARD DUTTON.—It is with regret we announce the death of Mr. Richard Dutton, formerly of West View, Lymm, at Cl2eve, near Bristol,which took place on Sunday morning. News reached Lymm on Sunday that he was very ill, and special prayers were offered on his behalf at theLymm Congregational Church on Sunday morning, bnt from news received on Mlnday it seems that he died during the hours of morning ser- vice. Mr. Dutton was formerly agent for the late Col. Fox's estate, and whilst he was a resident in Lymm was a leading spirit among the Lymm Congregationalists, and was also deservedly popular with all sections and creeds in the neighbourhood. He left Lymm about five years ago to live with his son, who had taken up his residence at Cleeve. He had served in several public offices, including that of a member of the Lymm Local Board. Mr. Dutton,who was a Liberal in politics, leaves a widow and three grown-up children.

GREAT SANKEY

SCHOOL BOARD MEETING.—The monthly meeting of the Greak Sankey School Board was held last evening in the Boardroom. There were present—alessrs. T. Halton J. Kilshaw, the Rev. E. S. Jackson, the Clerk (Mr. Jeans), and Mr. George Clarke (schoolmaster).—rhe Rev. E. S. Jackson said it had again fallen to his lot to propose a chairman for the Board. He might say that a membership OR the Board did not seem 3 to be coveted in the district. He had great pleasure in proposing Mr. Halton as chairman of the Board for the ensuing year.— Mr. Kilshaw seconded.—Mr. Halton said if he was in order he would propose that Mr. Jackson should take the chair.—Mr. Jackson said his health did not permit him, but he would be pleased to give the chairman assistance and support.—Mr. Halton said if it was their wish for him to take the chair he would be willing to do so, and do all he could for the welfare of the Board. He had not been a member of the Board before, and was quite new to the nature of the work. If he had the assurance of Mr. Jackson and the other member that they would give him all the support they could,he would be pleased to take the chair. (Flear,hear.)—Mr. Halton was then elected chairman.—The election of vice-chairman was left over until the next meeting.—Mr. Bate and Mr. Wright were elected to make up the number of the members of the Board subject to their consent.—A pre- cept was served on the overseers for £45,

William Jennings Bryan was originally employed 01 ' s." farm hand in Salem Illinois. He was always ah, worker, and entered with enthusiasm into all be did He earned his first dollar as water carrier, he being WI thirteen years old, and a strong and powerfully-built lata for his age. He invested his weekly earnings in boolat and he was a ravenous reader. Even at that early age he was a keen politician, and when the men were rest' log from their work in the shade of a fence youil Bryan would stand before them and declaim his viesiP on the political situation with energy and enthusiasra. He was always looked up to by the other farm-handh who always paid him a good deal more respect than hi° years warranted. Indeed, the others regarded him as man, and treated him as such.

The old woman," was in possession of all her faculties till the the last fel/ weeks, had been in the service of the Tanners throue five generations, and several members of the famili have photographs of representatives of their genertv tions with the centenarian in the midst of them. Mrs. Ball was sturdy ald strong and ac' tive down to four years age, when she fell down stela, but refusing all help, though much bruised, she piekell herself up and wonderfully recovered from the shock' She kept actively about tiil five weeks ago when het appetite gave way, and she declinnd to have her nightli glass of wkisky and water to which she had been acce tomed for the last quarter of a century. The Berlin correspondent of the " Standard " writeg —A highly interesting discovery, which will perbsPs afford a clue to the cause of cancer, has been made it! Professor Von Leyden's University H spital here. 1" young woman of 22 and a man of 63,both suffering fro' ascites and cancer of the stomach, were brought to the hospital last spring. In order to remove the accu nvi; lated fluid, tapping was resorted to in both cases. The fluid was examined under the microscope, and hitherto o unknown living organisms, belonging probably to tb_ protemoa,were discovered. Professor Waldayer,to vrh°lP the preparations were submitted, advised that tbe/. should be sent to Dr. Schaudinn,assistant at the Zootogi cal Institute of the Berlin University, who has been ir.i; vestigating the protozoa for years past. Fie began s examination on July 19, and ascertained in both case hich the fluid contained a parasitic aniceboid rhizopods 1 he named " Leydenia gemMipara Schauoinn.” ferred that it is a pathogenic organism, but both he an Professor Von Leyden still abstain from deciding as to the connection between it and cancer, though they ad- mit the possibility of such a connection. They hale made a preliminary communication on the subject to the Berlin Academy of Science.

At Havre on Tuesday evening a terrible accideeD occurred in a shop stocked with old iron, by which on! life was lost. Two young workmen named Louvel au° Lemesle, aged 17 and 14, entered the place for the pur pose of disposing of some iron barrel hoops. By sortie unfortunate mischance one of the youths picked up shell lying near at hand, and inadvertently let it droP; The projectile exploded with terrible foree, and LouVo was killed instantly. His companion's left arm w partially blown off, and had to be amputated on his 11_0: mittance to the hospital. A colonel in the artillery, well as the Mayor of Havre, have visited the scene 0; the accident, and examined the fragments of the shell' All are at a loss to explain how the explosive came to lying in the shop, but it is thought that it was bougin with a quantity of old iron, and that the purchaser di° net know the character of the dangerous article. The new Mannlicher magazine rifle (1895 pattero), adopted by the Austrian Government, is said to gilt' most satisfactory results. One hundred and fifty thoir sand of these weapons have already been issued to the troops. It is the lightest army rifle now in use, weigh' ing only 711 b.. while the old Austrian rifle weighed 91111: The barrel is encased in wood in its entire length, tnu, protecting it against damage by a fall or blow. Tbe bayonet as well vs its scabbard is also liehter than for' merly. The bore is the same as before, viz.,'3lsin., and the cartridges remain the same as for the previous pat' tern, so that the two weapons may be used together without any difficulty.

Apropos of prison reform, it is interesting to Imo/ that the mosticomfortable prison in the world is he 113 e United States. This is the Massachusetts Reformatorl at Concord, which, conducted on the " collegiot!, system," is, according to an authority, entitled to tho unique distinction, though its inmates include the avoro; class of criminals. Five years is the maximum perioa of detention, and even this may, by good behaviour, 13_,,,e reduced to eleven months. While incarcerated ther lectures on political, social, religious, trade, and Per, sonal subjects are delivered to the prisoners, mainly bY clergymen and professors, once or twice a week. The., there are classes for the study of music, politico' economy, mathematics, and architectural drawing, i° connection with the last of which the class execute free; hand designs in terra-cotta, plaster, and stucco, ow" also make designs for internal and external house deco' ration in wood, iron, stone, &c., with periodical exsl2r inations. In addition to the above there are also a debating society, a scientific and literary club, and 0, athletic asseciation, besides a farm of 100 acres works" by the prisoners, with several hundred cattle and pigs and a printing-office and weekly newspaper—the latter written, composed, and machined wholly in the " refer matory " itself. This may read like a romance, but 1G is a liceral fact. GREAT MEETING OF WIRE-

===== 0002601_18960912_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
DRAWERS.

PRESENTATION TO WARRINGION MEN,

Under the auspices of the Federated Wiredrawers' Society of Great Britain, a large demonstration was held at the Belle Vue Gardens, Manchester, on Saturday, when there were wiredrawers from all the principal districts of England present in large numbers. The foilowing were the districts repre- sented :—Halifax No. 1, Warrington, Birmingham, Sheffield, Halifax No. 2, and Middlesborough. Judg- ing from the numbers present, it is evident that the Society is a very powerful and influential one, for there were between 600 and 700 present besides wives of the members. This is the first ven- ture of the kind in the history of the Society, and the officials and committee are to be congratulated upon its complete success. The members were conveyed by special trains from the various districts to Manchester, and it is satisfactory to notice that the Warrington district which is one of the largest in point of member- ship, furnished a large quota of the total number, no fewer than 300 leaving the Central Station for Belle Vue at two o'clock in the afternoon, accompanied by Mr. Nathan Cooke, the chairman of the district, Mr. T. Wadsworth, general secretary of the federated Society, and Mr. B. Hall, the general treasurer. When they arrived at Belle Vue, they spent the time before tea in visiting the various parts of the gardens, and many re- unions of old friends who had parted for years to work in different districts were to be witnessed. A splendid knife and fork tea had been prepared for the excursion- ists in one of the dining rooms of the gardens, and at this there were over 600 present. Ample justice was done to the tea by the wiredrawers, and after tea, a meeting was held in the room, presided over by Mr. Henry Walker, of Birmingham, the chairman of the Society. At this meeting, short addresses were given by representatives of the various districts upon topics con nected with the Society, which we report below, and op portunity was also taken to present Mr. T. Howard, a wiredrawer, formerly working at the Whitecross wire- 7?rks,WarrinF.:ton,incapacitated from work,with £6O u- tilising the amount raised for him by a draw piomoted by the Society. Much stress was laid upon the beneficent work which could be done by the Society, as it was thus shown that the Society could,in a very tangible manner, help its members who from sickness, or old age were unable to continue in their employment. Mr. T. Wads- worth, of Warrington, was also presented with an illum- inated address in recognition of his valued services to the Society as its general secretary for a number of years. The proceedings of the meeting were very enthu- siastic from commencement to the finish.

===== 0002601_18960912_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MEETING.

As we have mentioned above, immediately after the I tea, a meeting was held, over which Mr. H. Walker, of Birmingham, presided. The Chairman at the outset expressed his pleasure at attending a gathering of that description. He said that he thought it was a unique gathering in the history of the Federated Wiredrawers' Society of Great Britain. (Applause.) He did not purpose saying very much, but he hoped that the meeting that day would lead to prac- tical results. It was through the actions of just men in the past that they were enabled to meet together en that occasion that day, and he hoped that they would emul- ate the deeds of those in days gone by, by striving to strengthen their cause still more. He was proud to say that they were stronger numerically and financially, as the balance sheet showed, than ever they were before—(applause)—but they must not rest on their oars, and be content with what they had done. They were able to accom- plish even greater things than they had done up to the present. The speaker then proceeded to speak of the movement in the Birmingham district, showing the satisfactory condition of things there. He said that their union and the employers were not necessarily antagonis- tic. They were willing to adopt new methods as they were presented to them, provided tt ey were not asked to do anything unreasonable. (Hear,hear.) At the pre- sent time there was only one dark speck in the horizon, and that was in the north. He could tell them that their executive council were determined to Gee' with that question. (Applause.) In conclusion, he expressed his confidence in the success of the organisation, and its value in promoting the interests of the workmen. Mr. Hemingway, of Halifax No. 1, was the next speaker. He said that he held very advanced views on trade matters. grades unionism was all right, but he thought that they lost one object in view, and that ob- ject was that they were combined together to protect their interest when the capitalists made any encroach- ments upon them. They lost sight of this when the general election came round, and often voted for the man they were organised against. He thought that they ought to be more careful how they recorded their vates. He was glad that their general secretary,Mr. Wadsworth was to have a testimonial at that Meeting, as he was in every way worthy of it. (Applause.) Mr. N. Cooke, chairman of the Warrington district, said it gave him great pleasure to have an opportun4y of saying a few words. He hopsd that that meeting would incite each one to work more earnestly as trades unionists than they had done in the past. The principle of trades unionism was to his mind, that whilst they were on the one hand looking after their own interests, they were looking also after •their master's interests, because a man,if he was a thorough trades unionist,and had trades unionism at heart,would strive to be the best workman possible for him to be. He carried a character with him, and he would help the master to support the prices in the trade he had worked at. (Applause.) There were men, he admitted, who were trades union- ists from selfish motives. There were other men who were not trades unionists' but he was glad to say that they were few and far between. As far as the Warrington district was concerned, they had very few men who reaped benefit from the trade who were not in their organisation. These few men, however, were living upon what the union had got for them. The unien had done the hard work and brought things to what they were at the present time, and then these men were living upon them, and taking what they in that room should have. (Hear,hear.) He thought that a de- termined effort should be made to get all men outside into their organization. There should be an increased incentive for bringing about a better state of opinion,as they had been prospering very much as far as.numbers were concerned, and they had done their duty in the Warrington district. (Applause.) During the last 12 months, while they had had increased members in that district, they had been looking round to induce members to join their society. They had succeeded in inducing those who had served their legal time to ask for admission into the Society. They had taken them in, and they had a strong evidence of their power by the large number present from Warrington that day. (Applause.) It was for that meeting to strengthen the hands of their officials, and one very good way of doing that was to keep up their contributions every week. He hoped that every one would pay them punctually and not get into arrears, and that from that meeting they would be stronger trade unionists than ever they were before. (Applause.) Mr. Arnold, of Birmingham was the next speaker, and he spoke of the great difficulties under which the Society was formed. The Society, he said, had passed through some dark days, but from 1886 onwards they had improved their position step by step, and the re- sult was that meeting there that day. (Applause.) In 1884 or 1886, it would have been considered impossible that such a meeting could have been got together within such a short space of time. But do not let them suppose that the work was ended. It was only partially done, and he hoped that those present that day would go back again and leave no stone unturned until they had suc- ceeded in getting all the men who were earning a living in the wiredrawing trade within the pale of their society. (Applause.) Mr. W. Sheffield, the Sheffield delegate, thought that. the meeting that day should have good results, and great benefit should accrue to the cause of trade union- ism. They were he said, met that day as members of the great trades union movement, and he did not think there was a man present who was not prepared be recog- nise that the general tendency of society to-day was towards collectivism and that collectivism was becom- ing more acknowledged day by day, and year by year. Trade unionism was only part and parcel of that great collective effort. He was in favour of the amalgamation of trades unionists with each other, and he appealed to the young men present to come to the assistance of the society and to raise it to an even greater height than it had occupied before. Mr. Lewis Ellison, as representative of the small wire drawers, in Halifax No. 2 district also expressed his s.ppreciation of the work of the Society in his district. Mr. Wright, the Middlesborough representati se, then addressed the meeting. He said that while they were willing to work for the masters, they wished to have a fair dar's work. (Applause.) Ile intended to belong- to the ranks of the trades unionists as long as he lived. in Middlesborough, they were able to hold their own against all the masters they met, and he assured the meeting that things would come round all right there in the end. (Applause.) PRESENTATION TO MR. T. HOWARD.

The Chairman said that they had on the programme one item which would commend itself to all who belonged to the organisation. That was an opportunity to help a fellow-workman in the Lime of sickness and adversity. (Hear, hear.) .1(1 had great pleasure in asking Mr. Jas. Crowther, of Warrin.4ton, to present Mr. Thomas Howard with the proceeds of the draw made for hire in connec- tion with the Society. (Applause.) Mr. J. Crowther said that the duty he had to perform was a most pleasing one. The various districts had done nobly for the object they had in view. (Hear, hear.) They had contributed manfully for the benefit of Mr. "rhomas Howard. The benefit of their members was always the object of the Society, and he returned his sincerest thanks to all. He was glad to tell them that they had realised the handsome sum ot £6o—(applause) —which would be of great benefit to Mr. Howard. While he thanked them all in general, he must thank Whiteoross in particular for their part. ro realise the £6O in the draw, it was necessary to raise £25, and out

of that Whitectbss had raised something Kke P.2o— BO per cent. They had taken a generous lead in the matter, acd Mr. Howard gave his heartfelt thanks for it. The money had been placed in the bank, and all he had to do was to hand Mr. Howard the book. He hoped that he would long live to have the benefit of the money. (Applause.) The book was then handed to Mr. Howard amid great applause, and Mr. Howard thanked Mr. Crowther for the gift. PRESENTATION TO MR. WADSWORTH.

The next business was to present to Mr. T. Wadsworth an illuminated address in acknowledgment of his ser- vices as general secretary to the Seciety. In making the presentation, the Chairman said that he was sure that everyone would agree with him that the presentation was well deserved by Mr. Wadsworth. (Applause.) /3e would read the words on the address, which raa as follows :—"Federated Wiredrawers' Society of Great Britain. This illuminated address is presented to Mr. Thomas Charles Wadsworth, by the Federated Wiredrawer's' Society of Great Britain, as a small token of respect and esteem for valuable and energetic services rendered to this Society during the past six years, as general secretary of the Society. At the same time, we hope and trust he may be spared a long and useful life in the grand and noble work of trades unionism." Then follow the names of officials and committee. Continuing, the Chairman said he had the greatest pleasure in mak- ing the presentation, and as one who had known Mr. Wadsworth he could speak of him as a most consistent, courageous, and reliable servant. (Applause.) He was a man who worthily occupied the position of general se- cretary,and he asked him to accept the address on be- half of the Society. (Applause.) In reply, Mr. Wadsworth said he would like in the first place to express his great pleasure in seeing so many wiredrawers from the various centres of the trade, assembled there that day. He was sure it must be also a great pleasure to those who suggested that a gathering similar to the present one should take place, with the view, if possible, of bringing them as workmen more in contact with one another. He might venture to say that the idea had been realised far beyond the ex- pectation of the most sanguine, and let them hope they would all spend a very pleasant day together, and the success of the meeting that day might be the fore- runner of pleasant meetings of the same character. (Applause.) Respecting the very handsome present which they hal thought fe: to make him he would like to say that he felt quite unable to express his thanks for the very handsome manner in which they had recogaised his humble abilities to serve them as General Secretary. Be must also not fail to express his appreciation of the honour they conferred upon him in re-electing him some short time ago unanimously ; and he could assure them he felt it to be a very great 'honour to be at the head of such a large body of men similar to what their Society was at present composed of. He might venture to say that so far as numerical and financial strength were con- cerned such a pitch had never before been attained in the history of the Society. But it did not follow that they should in anyway force themselves in conflict with any employers so long as they did what was right and reasonable, for if he understood the constitution of their Society aright, the main object was protection, and any- thing in the shape of conflict was a last resort. (Hear. hear.) He was pleased to be able to say that in all his dealings with the various employers he had had to meet as their representative, he had in all cases been treated with the greatest consideration and respect. As they knew there were times when the busi- ness was not of the most pleasant character,and the posi- tion rather an unenviable one. He had in the end had the utmost good feeling extended towards him ; and it was an advantage to all concerned that such a state of things should exist. (Hear, hear.) There was another pleasing feature in connection with their meeting, and that was the good run of trade they had had for some time past,which was a joint benefit both for themselves and the employers. He hoped it might long continue. Without taking up any more of the short time at their disposal, he asked them to allow him to agaia thank them heartily for their handsome recognition of his ser- vices on their behalf, and his earnest hope would be at all times, whether he was in office or out, to continue to enjoy that confidence of which their gift to him that day was a testimony. (Applause.)

Mr. Haigh, of the' Wirrington district, then proposed a vote of thanks to the chairman. He said that they had now learned the value of combination. He also claimed that a consistent trades unionist was a better workman than a non-unionist. (Applause.) The former wanted to make a name for himselt and therefore he was bound to do his work better. (Hear,bear.) He hoped that ths young men present would put themselves in harness as they would see the advantages which accrued from com- bination. He was pleased that they had been abl too recognise the services of Mr. Wadsworth who, he could. assure them, would keep the affairs of the Society in proper order. He had great pleasure in proposing a vcte of thanks to the chairman. (App!ause.) The vote was carried unanimously, and the chairman having briefly replied, the meeting terminated. The rest of the evening was spent in the gardens,and a little after nine o'clock the fire- works,reprcsenting the Battle of Alma, were shown with great effect. The re- turn home was made by special train at 10.30 p.m. and Warrington was reached about 11.30 after a most successful excursion.

?Letter to the Obitor.

LICENSING AT NEWTON.

Sir,—Will you kindly allow me to refer to the action of Mr. Deas, the manager of the Longford depot of the Warrington Corporation, in going before the licensing magistrates at Newton for the purpose of assisting in obtaining a drink licence in the immediate neighbour- hood of the depot. Mr. Dees has, of course, the right to hold his own opinion in regard to the drink or any other question, but in this case he gave evidence as the manager of the depot, that is to say, as an official of the Corporation, and therefore his conduct is a fair subject for thezriticism of any ratepayer. I should like to ask • -refore, whether Mr. Dees had the ahthority of his conimittfo to act in this important matte?, because I on of opirdon that a paid official of the 30r10rati0n has no right whatever to assume such a msporitiibility ? I should have thought that Mr. Deas was fully occupied with his duties as manager at Longford awl as water engineer, without troubling himself about toe means or Absence of means of entertaining the numet,as deputa- tions that visit Longford. works. It appear cit..° me that if Mr. Dees felt the necessity of providing tP.,,e_ieans of entertainment for the deputations. the naat* should have been brought to the notice of his committee by him, atui that they should have taken the matter in hand. It is irritating to a large section of the Warring- ton putolic to see any of its servants taking upon them- nelvte to speak in such matters in their name without I,beir Authority.—Yours truly. A LARGE RATEPAYLR.

AT WARRINGTON.

A WOMAN SHOCKINGLY MALTREATED.

HER LUSBAND COMMITTED FOR TRIAL. STARTLING DISCLOSURES.

Since Tuesday morning the town—a end more particu- larly:that part cf it known as Town's End—has been in a state of excitemest in consequence of a married woman named Margaret tate, wife of James Bate, living at 11, Oliv4r-street, beim; found dead in her bedroom with bruises about her face and body, and the disclosures which tuve since teen made resulting in the commit- ment of bir husbacd to the Liverpool Assizes on a charge of wiFul murder. It appears that both husband and wife who hee abotr. 52 'years of age, have been ad- dicted to drink, 41,1 it is stated that they have not lived very happily together. On Monday night the deceased woman was drinking in a neighbour's house and this seems to have exasperated her husband who found no tea provided for him us returning home from work. Be went in search of her ant tried to induce her to come home, but in vain. He appe•rs to have now proceeded to some public-house, and did not return home until about eleven o'clock when he we seen to be intoxicated. His wife had returned home in a smilar condition about an hour previously and proceeded ipstairs in company with the children to the bedroom a- the back. What happened when the husband got uptairs—if his little daughter's version of the affair is correct—is a sad story to relate, and the brutality used appears more like the actions of a madman. It is not necessary hire to repeat the child's evidence ; it will be found in fuli in the re- port of the Coroner's inquiry. She is a bright intelligent girl and made her statement in a clear and unmpitake- able manner. She was an eye-witness of what took plac, and was the first to go to the bedroom next morning where her mother had lain alone since the tragedy of the previous night. There is something very pathetic in the remark which she states she made to her father on running back to her room, " Mamma is very white." On finding that she was dead. Bate, his daughter save, sent for a bowl of water and a flannel, and washed the blood from his dead wife's face and also from the floor. The girl is very explicit in her statement as to her father suggesting to her that she should say her mother had sustained her injuries by falling downstairs. There seems little doubt that she did tell people such was the ease but she now maintains that it was untrue. Drs. Starkey Smith and Bowden who have made a post- mortem examination attribute death to an infusion of blood on the brain caused by direct violence. The pri- soner,who heard the evidence at the inquest,told several of the witnesses that they were telling lies. He seemed to be suffering acutely, and twice burst into tears. On hearing the verdict he completely broke down. He is a dark complexioned young man about 31 years of ago and of low stature. He has been employed at Messrs.H. D. Pochin and Co.'s Chemical Works, Bank Quay, whose works, it may be stated, are on the site vacated by Messrs. Lever Bros., the soap manufacturers. His mother who was in court during the inquest,interrupted the proceedings several times, and when the verdict of the jury was given she created a scene and was removed from the court. Within the precincts of the court a large crowd had assembled and waited!about during the three hours sitting of the jury,discussing the event. The result was known about twenty minutes to ten, and created a sensation',great excitement prevailing. It was sore time before the streets presented their usual ap- pearance again. POLICE COURT PROCEEDINGS.

At the Warrington Borough Court on Wednesday morning, before Mr. John Fairclough (chairmaia),Alder- man Wright, and Mr. John Geddes, James Bate was charged with feloniously and wilfully causing the death of his wife, Catherine Bate, sometime between Monday night and Tuesday morning. The Chief Constable said he would ask for a remand until Monday next, in order that the evidence might be made complete. A post-mortem examination would be made that morning, and he was not in a position to call medical evidence at present, and in several particulars the evidence was not perfect. But this was the evidence so far as it had at present been collected by the police. The prisoner and his wife had been married for about 14 years. He was a labourer in a chemical works, and for the last three weeks the husband and wife had lived in a cottage, No. 11, Oliver-st., in the neighbourhood of Orford-lane. They appeared for a number of years to have been of intemperate, even it he did net say of drunken habits, and to have lived practically a cat and dog life,since quarrels were,unfortunately, very freqeent between them as the neighbours and children could tell them. On Monday last the woman,instead of attending to her household duties, appeared to have gone into the house of a neighbour named Conner, and there she and several other women passed the time in drinking beer fuddling, in fact, in a private house. The man came home from his work shortly after six o'clock, and went in search of his wife and saw her in the house, but he did not induce her to return with him. He was wrath, and having spoken in the street to his daughter, who is 12 years of age, be went away. He did not return to the neigh- bourhood until alter eleven o'clock. The deceased reached home about ten o'clock, and she was then un- doubtedly and unmistakably,according to the testimony of several witnesses, considerably irtoxicated. She went to bed with her daughter, to whom she made a sienificant remark, "Maggie, go and get me a nail and a e -mmer, in order that I may fasten the door, so that your _faddy cannot come to me and lay on me." She (the deceased) went to bed, not to the one she ordin- arily occupied with her husband, but to the bed in the back bedroom, which contained nothing else than a paillasse, which was on the floor. At about eleven o'clock the prisoner was seen coming along 011iver-et. in an intoxicated condition. He was singing. He went into his house, and what transpired there would be told them in the words of the little girl, who was the only ccmpetent witness of the dreadful scene which ensued. Bate shouted uptairs to his wife, " You have no supper ready for me," but his wife made no reply. He thee went upstairs wearing the strong heavy boots (now produced) and asked her to come into her own bedroom, but she refused to do so, and the Bench would be told that the prisoner then struck her a violent blow in the face with his fist. Not content with that,he kicked the woman once in the face violeutly,once in the stomach and once upon one of her legs while she was in bed. The peor woman cried out "On ! Jim, don't lay on me, and I v ill give you 2id. for a pint of beer." She then turned over on her face, when the prisoner stamped upon her back with his heavily shod feet. She then cried out "Oh ! Jim, you have murdered me," and he said " Yes, thot is what I intend to de." That was plainly overheard by the neighbours in the two adjoining houses. The house was only separated by a single brick, so that every sound could be heard by the persons living on each side ol it. The prisoner then appears after his wife' had said "Oh ! Jim, you have murdered me," to have caught hold of der by the throat with both hands, and, as the little girl said, he choked her until she became quiet. He took the little girl and the other children in- to the front room and lay on the bed, and the children fell asleep. The neighbours, however, heard him ravine in a drunken manner and using foul language until about three o'clock in the morning, and tbe foul lan- guage was directed against the wife. Singular as it might seem, the man slept in the house all night with the dead body in another room. When the daughter awoke next morning he said to her " Go into the other room and fetch your skirt." She did so, and noticed that her mother was lying on her back with her arms stretched out. Her eyes were wide open and she looked very white. She went back to her father and said "Mamma is very white." The husband then went in- to the room, raised his wife's head, and said to his daugh- ter "She is dead. You must say she tumbled downstairs, or I shall be locked up." The little girl was frightened, and she did do what she was told at first to seme people who asked for particulars as to the death of her motLer. Now she entirely repudiated the state- ment, and had told the whole truth. She was a singu- larly bright and intelligent girl. The prisoner then told the little girl to bring him a dish and some water and a flannel. She did so, and she would tell the Bench later on that he washed the blood off the dead face of his wife, also the blood from the floor. The dish and the water were taken downstairs, and the flannel wee now in the custody of the police and would tell its own:story. He thee went acress to a neighbour's house, Mrs. Brown's—and this was a most singular proceeding— and said to'her "I ant in a bit of trouble, Kate is dead," but Mrs. Brown did not appear to have asked why a recently healthy woman had come to such a sudden death. She went with him to the house and assisted him to remove the dead body from the back room into the front bedroom, where it was afterwards seen by the police. He then told Mrs. Brown that he would "go and give the alarm to the police." That was between nine and ten o'clock in the morning, but the " alarm " was never given.

The Prisoner : No, sir, but- The Chief Constable : Steady a moment. Just about that time he was met by Mrs. Kelly, the mother of the deceased, who had heard of the death, and she said to him '• Jim. what is a miss with Kate ? Is she killed ? I hear she is kißed. You have killed her." He said "No, it's a --tie, I have not. She was drunk and:fell down- stairs." But that could not be true in the face of the masa of evidence they had from the littl.e girl and the

neighbours. Information of the crime reached the police I about ten o'clock, and Sergeant Cheney went to the house and found the deceased lying on the bed with I shocking bruises on her face and head and other parts of I her body. He saw that there wasievidence of foul play, and he sought for the prisoner. He found him in Buckley-street about two o'clock in the afternoon, brought him to the police station, and cautioned him in , the usual form. He was told that anything he might say would be taken down in writing, and that it might be given in evidence against him, and this was the statement he made :—"I have to say that I had nothing to do with it at all. When I came home last night it has been the regular thing for three months for her to be drunk every Monday night, and when I entered the house the things were on the table, and the house empty. I came out and went across to Mrs. Brown's and asked one of the girls where Kate was. They told me she was in Connor's drunk. I went there and found her drunk, in company with a lot more women. They were all drunk, and snuff running down their faces. They were drinking beer. I walked away. I never saw my wife from then till this morning when my daughter woke me about eight o'clock, and asked me to come and look at her mother, as she looked very white. It was on the advice of Mrs. Shaw that I came up Buckley- street to get my mother to accompany me to the police station. I was arrested."

Sergeant Cheney corroborated the statement of the Chief Constable. He said he arrested the pridoner in liuckley-strelt. He was wearing the heavy boots now produced. The Chief Cohstable : On that I ask for a remand. The Chairman (to prisoner) : Have you anything to say why you should not be remanded ? The Prisoner : Yea, I want to ask the little girl a question or two. The Deputy-Clerk : That will come later on. The Chairman : Have you anything to say against a remand?

Prisoner : No, sir. The Chairman : Then you will be remanded until Monday. The Chief Constable asked that the prisoner might be kept in the cells below. The man had no objection, and it would be as fair for him as for the prosecution in case Bate wanted to obtain assistance.

This was agreed to. Prisoner : I should like to speak about ray little daughter. rhe Chrirman : You will have plenty of time to do that. The prisoner was then romoved to the cells.

===== 0002601_18960912_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
VIEWBG THE BODY.

Prior to the inquest the jurymen assembled in the Parade Room, Irlam-street, where they were met by the Coroner. He explained to them that he had arranged for them to be taken in a conveyance to the house of the deceased woman and perhaps if he said something now of the occurrence it would save anything further at the house. According to the police rep-rt, the woman Catherine Bate came home on Monday night and went to bed in one of the rooms of the house. The husband came and found she was not sleeping in the room where she usually slept but with the children. It was suggested that he went to that room and committed violence which caused her death,and having done so he went to the other room and left her there until the next morning. What took place was heard by some nighbeurs, and he wished the jarymen to take particular notice of the two rooms, and they might also experiment and see whether it was possible for the neighbours to hear what took place. The jury then pre- ceeded to the house 11, Oliver-F,t. ,in an omnibus, pre- ceded by the Coroner and the Chief Constable in a han- som. As is probably well-known,Oliver-st.is a by-street from Winwick-road, and the neighbourhood is one the poorest parts of the town. The house consists of four rooms, two up and two down. It was found to be almost devoid of furniture. In the back bedroom there was nothing but a bed on the floor, and it is in this room where it is alleged the woman met with her death. On the wall, in which was a small fire-grate, the Chief Constable showed bloodstains on the wall and also on the floor. In order to test whether it was possible for the next door neighbour to hear what was taking place in the room, Inspector Spinks was sent there, and his voice could be distinctly heard through the wall,which it may be stated is built only of a single course of bricks, or known as a 4iin. wall. The bruises on the dead woman's face were of a prominent character, and the doctor's report on their examination of the body, was read, and the marks indicated were examined by the jurymen. After a brief stay, the journey to the Police Station was com- menced and the inquest was held in the Police Court, which was found far more convenient than the parade LOOM.

THE INQUEST.—VERDICT OF WILFT.IL MURDER. Mr. S. Brighouse, county coroner, and a special jury conducted an inquiry relative to the death of the woman on Wednesday evening. The proceedings commenced shortly after 6.30, and lasted over three hours. Mr. Fanshawe was the foreman of the jury. The Coroner addressed the prisoner, and told him that the jury were going to inquire into the cause of the death of his wife. He would have an opportunity e f asking the witnesses anything he liked at the proper time,and as he (the Coroner) had told him that morning when he had an interview with him, there were three courses open to him. First, he could tell the jury that he did not wish to say anything, or he could make a statement about the matter, but if he did it would be taken down in writing, and might be given in evidence against him or otherwise. Ana now this was very im- portant. lie could at that stage of the proceedings offer himself as a witness, and give evidence on oath. Thet was the only opportunity the law. gave him of doing so. If he were sent for trial by that jury, neither before the committing justices nor before the judge of assize, if the case got so far, would he have an opportunity of giving evidence. The first witness called was Margaret Kelly, who stated that she was the wife ef Patrick Kelly, of 30, Foundry-st., a general labourer. The deceased was her daughter and was 33 years of age. She last saw her alive a week ago, and saw her dead on Tuesday morn- ing about ten o'clock. She had been married 14 years, and there were four children living. Their names were Margaret 12 years old, John 4, Lizzie 4 (twins), and Kitty 2 years. She was just going to see her when she met the prisoner corning out of Mrs. Brown's who lived on the opposite side of the street to 11 Oliver-st., where the deceased lived. She said to the prisoner " Jim, what in the world is matter this morning with you and with Kate, she's dead?" He said "I don't know; she was drunk and she fell downstairs." She then said "Well Jim your long threatenings have come at last. You have many a time said you would do it, and you have done it at last ; you've killed her." Re said "You are a liar; I havn't killed her, she fell downstairs." He then went off and said he was going fora policeman and she would see whether he was telling lies or not. He went down street and she never saw him again until he was in custody. She went into the house and saw her daughter dead. She was lying in the front bedroom. Ellen Brown, wife of Walter Brown, general labourer, said she lived at 18, Oliver-street. She had been on friendly terms with the Bates. They had lived there for three weeks. Mrs. Wheelan, widow, living next door to Bate's house said she knew the deceased. She had had no acquaint- ance with the prisoner. She saw the deceased on Mon- day night under the influence of drink. Elizabeth Shaw, datiehter of the last witness, said she was a widow. The evidence given was quite correct. She had no acquaintance with the prisoner or his wife. door to the deceased's houee), said he heard the prisoner in his house from eleven o'clock to twelve on Monday. He heard the prisoner say " Where is my b-- supper ?" He heard him go upstairs and use baci language. He also heard some bumping after that, and afterwards heard him say "Come Maggie to bed." It sounded as if he had his boots on.—gy the Chief Constable: Did you hear the woman say anything ?—Yes, she said Oh ! four times.—By the Prison ee : Where were you at the time. Witness : Yes.—Prisoner : I don't believe that man was in the house at the time.—The Coroner: The prisoner says he doesn't believe you were in the house P—Wit- ness : I was in bed at a quarter to eleven.—Prisoner: How is it your wife could not hear it ?—She might have been asleep. Ido not know.—Prisoner : You ought to be made to know.—Witness: My wife told me that she was frightened to tell you anything. Dr. Starkey Smith, physician, said he made a post- mortem examination that morning assisted by Dr. Bowden of Warrington. The report in the Coroner's hands had been written by him.—The Coroner then read the report which stated that the body was that of a well nourished woman. There was an external wound on the forehead three inches in diameter. The tip of the nose was depressed and discoloured ; there was a eon- tused wound on the left side of the chin—three-quarters to one inch long—and a contusion below the left ear. There were also numerous bruises on tke legs and knees, bruises on the left buttock,abrasions of wrist and middle finger of right hand,and large bruises on the right fore- arm. On removing the me'nbrane covering the brain the interior portion was seen to be in an advanced state of congestion but no rupture of artery or laceration of brain substance was found. On removing the brain tke congested condition previously mentioned was found to extend to the base of the brain, and just at the juncture of the brain and the spinal cord, a slot of blood, the size of,say, half a walnut, was found. The cause of death was an effusion of blood on the brain caused by direct violence (slight commotion).—By the Coroner : Sup- pose for the sake of argument doctor, that the woman fell downstairs and that the effusion of blood was the result,do you think she would be able to walk upstairs and get into bed before she became unconscious ?—No, sir.—Do you think the effusion of blood would be rapid in bringing on unconsciousness ?—I think so ; it is a very vital part.—Snppose she is going to the closet, as had been sugeested,a.nd slipped in the yard and broeght about this effusion of blood ; do you think she would have sufficient time to walk upstairs to bed ?—I think not,sir.—You have heard the evidence given by the daughter of the deceased ?—I have.—lf that is true was the violence sufficient to produce what you found?—Ye? sir, I may say on the legs we found some old bruises, but about the knees, particularly the left knee, there were four or five distinct fresh bruises. There was another on the right knee but the left knee seemed to be more injured. There was also a wound on the back of the left hip. The injuries on the face were all fresh.— By the Chief Constable: If the woman was kieked with the boots produced would you expect to find her in the condition you saw her P—lf the front part of the boot were used it would, but if the tip had caught her we should nave found more evidence of cutting. He thought the depression and disoolouration of the nose was due to a violent blow with the fist, but the injury to the fore- head had been done with something more like a boot. The effusion of blood had been brought about by direct violence. He expected there had been some laceration of a small vessel, and the blood had trickled down to the back of the brain. He thought she could not live very long after that clot of blood was formed, or per- haps he should say after the blood was effused. She would probably die before the blood was clotted.—By the Chief Constable : Did you notice her teeth ? No sir, Mr. Bowden examined the mouth carefully.—A jury- man : I suppose it would be impossible for her to live an hour after the effusion of blood P—Undoubtedly. Dr. Bowden said he assisted at the post-mortem examination. He had heard Dr. Smith's evidence, and he agreed with him as to the cause of death.—The Coroner : You know the two theories set forth by the Prisoner ; one that she fell downstairs and the other that she fell in the yard; do you think it possible for her to get back to bed again before becoming unconscious? —He would not say it was impossible,but it was unlikely. He examined the mouth, but did not find anything wrong with the teeth. There was some blood in the mouth, but that might have come from the nostrils.— By the Chief Constable: The violence in this case would be very considerable? Yes.—Tlae Foreman of the jury: The woman being intoxicated, would that cause her death sooner ?—lt is said there are some vessels likely to give way sooner when the person is subject to drink,but the deceased showed very little signs of drink.—By Mr. Evans (a juryman) : Do you think it possible that the injuries might have been caused when she fell in the street as stated by one at tbe witnesses ?—I clout think it likely. Sergeant Cheney stated that in consequence of information received he wart in search of the prisoner. He found him at two o'clock in the afternoon in Buckley- street. He was then walking towards him. He did not believe that the prisoner had been to the Police Station to report the death of his wife. He made enquiries at the office and found that he had not done so. He charged the prisoner with wilfully causing the death of his wife, Catherine, between 11 and 12 p.m. on Monday, September 7th. He made the following reply : (Ser- geant Cheney then read the reply as given in the police court). This concluded the police evidence. The Prisoner was then allowed to go into the witness box and was addressed by the Coroner, se ho said he wanted the prisoner to thoroughly understand the posi- tion he was in. Having heard the evidence he (the Coroner) should direct the jury that they were bound to send him for trial. They had no other alternative but to do so on a charge of having caused the death of the deceased. That being so the prisoner could say he had nothing to say, or make a statement or offer himself as a witness and be sworn. If he took the latter course he would have to be subject to crosa-examination. That was all the law compelled him (the Coroner) to tell the prisoner. He did not want the prisoner to do anything prejudicial to his own interests and he thought he was only doing right, when he stated that if he was acting as the prisoner's legal adviser, he should advise him not to make any statement. The Prisoner: I don't properly understand what you say, as lam ignorant of some of the words you use. If I had someone to speak for me and offer me advise, it would be better for me. I want a counsel to speak for me.

The Chief Constable : Yes, that will take place when you come up for trial. The Coroner: I should advise you to do what I have said. What you have already said has been against yourself and not in your favour. I don't say you would have been in a better position if you had not done se but you would have been in as good a position. The Prisoner : I was obliged to speak for myself. I had none to speak for me. 'the Coroner: We understand that you think they have been telling lies. The Prisoner was then led back to his original place where he sat crying bitterly. The Coroner then summed up to the jury and said they had to decide the matter. He thought they would agree with him that in dealing with the prisoner he (the Coroner) had been as impartial as possible and tried to see that he had had fair play, that nothing was said to prejudice the case, and the prisoner's position properly explained to him. There was no necessity to burden them with any lengthy remarks with regard to the facts. The issue was quite clear and plain. They had to say first of all did they think this woman died from injuries re- ceived. When they came to consider that point they had to bear in mind the evidence of Dr. Smith and Dr. Bowden, who told them that she died from an effusion of blood on the brain, the result of direct violence. If they believed the medical evidence, then they had the foundation of their finding. If they found that she died as stated by the medical gentlemen, the first question was, hew did the violence come about ? Was it received at the hands of her husband or in some other fashion ? If they believed that the violence was not administered by her husband there was an end of the matter so far as he was concerned. But when they c +me to consider whether he used that violence or not, to his mind they had to clear those three points in their mind :—First, his conduct before the alleged violence, secondly, the evidence as to the violence,and thirdly, his conduct after the alleged violence. As to the first point it was quite clear that rightly or wrongly the prisoner was much annoyed that his wife was not in when he came for his tea. It they believed the evidence of a neighbour when she came home, he was very mach annoyed at that particular time. He was there with no good feel- ings towards her and it was quite clear he was wretched and felt he ought to say something to her or do some- thing to her. Tnen the daughter had explained what took place in the bedroom, and he was certain that in their experience—for he knew that even in his experi- ence—they had not had a more clear and concise state- ment of facts that from that little girl. The cross-ex- amination by her father,the examination by yourselves and myself to see whether she was telling the truth ended in her triumph. She came out of the ordeal far better than most people,and he thought she went out of the witness-box having said everything that she believed to be true. If they believed she did so, they must be- lieve that the prisoner in the heat of a passion went into the bedroom and struck his wife,kicked her,stamped her down with his feet and knocked her head on the floor in the way described. The doetcrs said that was the kind of violence to produce the effusien of blond on the brain. Putting this toeether he asked them to say whether they had any doubt that the violence was used by the hus- band. The girl stated that when the prisoner saw the next morning what had happened he asked her to tell lies. fie suggested that his wife had fallen downstairs but the question was whether it was not improbable that this kind of violence could have caused what they had heard happened. If they were satisfied that the prisoner used violence which caused her death, then they were entitled to take into consideration the prisoner's conduct alter the death. If the woman had died in an innocent fashion didn't they think that the first thing the prisoner would have done would have been to go to the Police Station and report it ? On the contrary from nine o'clock to two, he had wandered somewhere in the berough. He did rot come to the

police station until arrested at two o'clock. If he did not commit the violence, was there any reason why he should do that ? Was there not every reason to think that knowing he had committed the violence he was try- ing or attempting to forget the result, not knowing what to do under the circumstances. If they were satis- fied that the woman's death was through violence there was no doubt that the prisoner killed his wife, and the question would arise in what degree he should go for trial. Now all the law said was that if any one killed another unlawfully, the person killing had committed wilful murder unless there were circumstances of justi- fication, extenuations, or reasons which in the minds of those trying the question justified them in saying that it should be reduced from wilful murder to manslaughter. But the duty of proving the latter rested with the per- son accused and not with the prosecution. It was for the person who was charged with the offence to show that he was justified in doing what he did for them to reduce the crime from marder to manslaughter. Let them take the excuse that the prisoner was drunk. He (the Coroner) had no doubt that he was. It might be that the next morning he was not aware of what he had done. It might be that he had so much drink, that he was in such a degree of passion that his mind was a total blank as to what had happened until his daughter told him that his wife was lying white and pale. But that was no justification because if they were to say a man wasn't to be responsible for what he did when drank, any man might fill his body with drink, take advantage of his drunken state and not be held responsible for what he did. Therefore it would be a wrong conclusion to draw that because a man was drunk he was entitled to have his offence reduced. They must try the prisoner and his acts as if he was a sober man. So far as her condition went it could not be justification for him to do what he did, er because she did not get his tea ready whee she was drunk. He could not say that it was an excuse that his wife was drunk. If that was so every person might set up the &acne plea. He directed them that there was nothing to justify them in reducing the charge. There was no distinct explanation or reasons given. If they believed she died from violence administered by her husband it was their duty to send him on a charge of wilful murder. Let them bear in mind they were not sitting there as a jury to decide whether he was guilty or not guilty. They were sitting there as if they were a committing magistrate discussing whether there was a prima facia case and whether he eheuld go for trial. He asked them whether there was any doubt that there was a prime facia case, or that the prisoner ought to go for trial on the capital charge. The jury then retired to consider their verdict, and after deliberating in private for about half-an-hour, returned a verdict of " Wilful murder against the prisoner." The prisoner, who had been much agitated until the verdict was returned, was then put into the box and the Coroner addressing him said : The jury have come to the conclusion that your wife died by violence adminis- tered by yourself, and that you wilfully murdered her. On that charge of wilful murder you will take your trial at the next Assizes to be holden at Liverpool. On hearing the result the mother of the prisoner created a scene, and shouted out that the deceased was a woman" who had been murdered by a great many." She was promptly removed from the court. The Prisoner : "It is only six weeks ago that her father put six stitches in her head. He daresay Dr. Bowden attended her."—He then became much dis- tressed, and was sobbing loudly when tbe court was being cleared.

LOYAL FRIELDSHIP LODGE OF ODDFELLOWS.

The fortnightly meeting of this lodge was held on Monday at the Green Dragon Inn, Bridge-street. P.G,M. Thomas Knight presided, supported by the following officers :—Prov. C.T. Joseph Crosby, P.P.G.M. Henry Longshaw, P.G. Daniels, P.G. Wood, P.G. Johnson, P.G. Dickenson, and Secretary John Burgess. The last-named called upon the chairman to present a medal to P.P.G.M. Henry Longshaw as a token of respect on his retirement as senior trustee of the lodge. Bro. Longshaw responded in suitable terms, after which the lodge was closed in the usual manner. Two young gentlemen drove out of Bridge-street on Sunday evening into Rylands-street,and when a few yards from the Royal Court Theatre the horse suddenly became unmanageable and began to plunge about. The driver jumped into the street, and no sooner had he done so than the horse made a rush towards Mr. Johnson's shop, backed into the middle of the street, threw itself on its hind legs and fell backwards between the shafts, its head resting on the road near to the splashboard. The other occupant of the trap had in the meantime jumped out from behind, and both be and the driver, fortunately, escaped unhurt. Strange to say the shafts were not broken,but the trap was slightly damaged at the back. When it fell on its back the the horse remained perfectly still for a few minutes. Then a number of men from Bridge-se. rushed up and liberated it. Later on in the evening the horse performed some most marvellous feats, and quite kept up its reputation. After leaving Warrington it bad a great desire to mount the rails into the Ship Canal. Not content with this it tried to de- posit the occupants of the trap there. Several times the animal backed into the hedge, and on one occa- sion one of the party did a most marvellous per- formance by turning a somersault over a hedge and alighting in an adjoining garden, none the worse for the acrobatic feat. The other occupant of the trap was precipitated over the horse's head.

WOUNDING CASES AT HAYROCX AND GOLBORNE. At the Warrington Occasional Court on Monday morning, before Mr. Ziba Armitage, Absalom tfarter was charged with having on the sth September, at Haydock, unlawfully wounded Mary Ann Cunliffe, by kicking her.—P.C. Christy said that at three p.m. on the sth September, from information he received, he proceeded to the complainant's dwell- ing-house where he saw complainant lying on the sofa in the kitchen. She was bleeding profusely from the mouth and nose, and also complained to him that the defendant had been kicking her about her arms and legs. Defendant came in whilst wit- ness was there. Witness was shown the injuries, and he found that she was "black and blue all over." Dr. Oscar had examined her. Witness ap- prehended the defendant at Haydock and took him to the police station, and charged him with the offence to which he replied "I was not in the house at the time ; I did not touch her." The complain- ant was not in a fit state to appear. Defendant said he would reserve his defence. He was the victim of a conspiracy.—The magistrates then remanded him —Absalom Pimblott, 77, Hedge Green-lane, Golborne, was charged with hav- ing wounded Ann Roberts on the sth inst. with intent to do grievous bodily harm.— Mr. Stuart, Wigan, appeared for the defendant.— Charles Williams, a bricklayer, residing at Hedge Green-lane, Golborne, said that at balf-pat ten he saw the prisoner at 73, Hedge Green-lane. He came to witness's house and commenced to make a noise. He jumped on a broom and broke it. He then knocked the door open and Ann Roberts was struck on the head with a piece of mug, which made her inbensible. She was a cousin of the witness. Com- plainant bled profusely from the wound.—Mr. Stuart, for the defence, said according to the wit- ness there appeared to be no intention to hurt Oomplainant. They had no evidence that the woman was dangerously burt.He had two substan- tial sureties for the man's bail.—This case was also remanded.

ST. THOMAS'S YOUNG MEN'S CLASS.—On Saturday afternoon a fishing match was held in connection with the above class. About 30 joined in the competition and the total number of fish caught was 36. NEW POLICE COURT MISSIONARY.

At the monthly meeting of the Executive of the Diocesan C.E.T.S. held on Thursday, Mr. Charles Wm. Harris of Lichfield was appointed Police Court Missionary for the Wigan Petty Sessions, Newton- le-Willows, &c., and will commence work at once. Mr. Harris who is 31 years of age has had consider- able experience in temperance work in India and England, and comes with the highest references. There were 61 applications. Mr. Taylor the W ar- rington Missionary will thus be able to devote more time to Warrington and Widnes.

But a Mend whom I met in town told me I would be sure to see Effingham up here. He has made dozen visits to Cromer since his wife died—" " A h ! " said the baron, again breathing heavili. "Of course there's no harm in the colonel's coming up to Cromer. He told one of the fellows at the club that Cromer was a bit of the original Paradise, and that he had found his Eve there. Effingham must be a romantic fellow. His Eve in a Norfolk paradise I firs ha ! " The stab had been dealt, and the false Wend watched anxiously yet furtively to see Its effect. Lord Cho.mpney's eyes blazed, and his face groW strangely white. The stroke had gone home to his very heart. Yet he was too proud a man to openlY show his hurt. "Effingham may have fallen in kw, with some Norfolk fisher beauty, or with a farmer's daughter," he said, with a quivering smile. "How be can stay in dull Cromer passes my comprehen- sion." "it's a paradise to b(,n now that he is in love," smiled Warner, well satisfied with the result of his nefarious work. "He may call here when he finds you arc at home." Lord Champney's face grew stern. He arose and moved towards the door. "I must go below, Felix," he said. "Come down when you are ready. It's near the luncheon hour." He hurried into the corridor, feeling strangely "So Effingham comes often to Cromer," he mut- tered, his jealous passion all ablaze. "I have come home just in time. I can fanoy who the Eve is who tempts him up here' although Felix had no suspicion. I shall watch Barbara like a lynx and if—. Oh, Barbara, my wife, false as fair ! *hat is to be the end of aft this? Shall I, like Samson, perish, bringing with me Barbara and those she falsely loves into a common rain ?" (To be continued.)

WEEKLY REPORT ON THE HEA.LTH OF WARRINGTON. 1." During last week, which was the 34th, of the current year, the following births and deaths were registered : Births. Deaths. Warrington 25 18 Latchford Total 27 20 The excess of deaths over births was 7. The death rate from all causes was equal to 17.7 per I,ooo,against 24.1 the average of the previous four weeks, and against 28.2 the corresponding week of last year. The death rate from the seven chief zyinotic diseases was equal to 5•3 per 1,000,. against 60 the average rate of the four previous weeks,and against 10.0 the corresponding week of 1895. The ages of mortality were- - 1 1-5 5-15 15-25 25-65 65 up. 5 3 3 2 5 2-20 20 The deaths included-1 from enteric fever, 5 from diarrheea, 2 from phthisis, 4 from heart-disease, 2 from ung disease, 2 from injuries. The foil Jiving cases of infectious disease have been reported during the week :—enteric- fever 1 case. Total number of patients in hospital on August 22.-12 JOHN H. GORNALL, Medical Officer of Health.
